# Practitioner review packet — SmartCiti.X ~VR Simulators and Trade Skills Simulator

_Generated by `node tools/gen_review_packet.mjs` from the station modules; regenerate after any content change. Do not edit by hand — record verdicts in the sign-off tables and commit, or transcribe them to the tracking system._

**Purpose.** Every station here states a real union trade and a real certification or standard, and scores a learner against an ordered procedure with seeded hazards. Before any training record from a station is treated as evidence of readiness for that certification, a qualified practitioner in the trade reviews the station: is the order of operations right, is every rationale true, is every hazard's consequence realistic, and is the certification claim the right one? This packet is the review document. One section per station, with a sign-off block.

**Two signatures per section.** The first is automated and already present: a *preview* verdict from the generator confirming the section is complete and internally consistent (category, trade and certification named; a rationale on every step; a judgeable consequence on every hazard; late notes on real targets; the scene builds inside the headset mesh budget; every dossier statement sourced). The second is the practitioner's, and it is blank until a named person signs it. A preview pass is the entry ticket to review, not a stand-in for it.

**How to review.** Play the station once (`smartcity/index.html?sim=<id>` or `trades/index.html?room=<id>`), then work down its tables. Mark each step's rationale and each hazard's consequence yes or no with a note. Sign the block. A station with any "no" is not approved until the change is made and the section regenerated.

**Roster.** 59 stations and rooms across 13 categories. Automated preview passed on 59/59 on 2026-09-18. Practitioner sign-off: 0/59.

# Energy & Power

## Charge Point (SmartCiti.X · `charge-point`)

**Category:** Energy & Power  
**Trade:** EV service technician  
**Certification / standard claimed:** IBEW — NFPA 70E arc-flash qualified, EVITP-certified EV infrastructure technician  
**Tagline:** DC fast-charger fault isolation, capacitor discharge and busbar torque  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the work order and fault code | Select | The fault code tells you which module and which isolation points. Walking up and opening doors is how you isolate the wrong cabinet. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | End the charging session | Select | A controlled stop ramps the current to zero and unlocks the connector. Everything after this assumes no load on the cable. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Establish the work zone | Sequence (any order: cone at the bay entry, cone at the kerb side, barrier across the bay) | A charging bay is a live traffic lane. The work zone stops a driver reversing onto you while your head is inside a cabinet. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Don class 0 gloves and face shield | Select | Class 0 rubber gloves with leather protectors and a face shield, before the door opens. The DC link does not care that the AC supply is off. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Open the upstream AC disconnect | Select | Isolate at the source, not at the charger's own contactor. A control-level stop is not an isolation. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Lock and tag the disconnect | Select | Your lock, your key. A charger that reboots itself while your hands are inside is a charger someone else energised. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Wait out the DC link discharge | Timed hold | The bleed resistors need their stated time. Counting to ten and opening the panel is how technicians find out what a charged DC link feels like. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Prove the DC link is dead | Graded reading | Absence of voltage is measured, not assumed. Below the manufacturer's safe threshold — anything else and you wait longer. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Withdraw the faulty power module | Select | Modules are hot-swap rated in service, not in fault. With the link proven dead you can handle it without the arc risk. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Torque the busbar connection | Graded reading | Under-torqued busbars run hot and eventually weld themselves; over-torqued ones crack the lug. The number on the label is the number. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Restore and test-charge | Select | The job is not finished at the repair. A test session under load is what proves the fix and clears the unit back to service. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `dc-busbar` | That is the DC link busbar. The supply being open means nothing here — the capacitors hold hundreds of volts for minutes after shutdown, and they will not let go once you are across them. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `damaged-cable` | That cable has a cut through to the screen. A damaged DC lead on a 350 kW charger is not a cosmetic fault; bin it and tag the unit out of service. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `interlock-bypass` | That jumper defeats the door interlock. Every bypass ever fitted 'just for testing' is still fitted. The interlock is the last thing standing between the next technician and the DC link. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `live-connector` | The vehicle is still in a charging session. Pulling a CCS connector under load draws a DC arc that will not self-extinguish — stop the session at the vehicle or the charger first. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Solar Deck (SmartCiti.X · `solar-deck`)

**Category:** Energy & Power  
**Trade:** Solar / BESS technician  
**Certification / standard claimed:** IBEW — NABCEP PV Installation Professional certified  
**Tagline:** Rooftop array and battery commissioning: fall protection, rapid shutdown and string test  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Survey the roof and anchor plan | Select | You establish where the anchors are and where the unprotected edges start before you step onto the deck, not while you are on it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Connect to the anchor | Select | The anchor is rated and inspected. Connecting to a vent stack or a pipe because it is closer is how anchors fail at the worst moment. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Don and check the harness | Select | The harness is inspected at every use. A frayed lanyard passes right up until the day it does not. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Initiate rapid shutdown | Select | Rapid shutdown drops the array conductors to a safe voltage outside the modules. It is the only thing that makes the roof wiring approachable in daylight. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Open the DC disconnect | Turn | DC first. Opening AC while DC is still feeding the inverter leaves stored energy and a live front end behind the cover. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Open the AC disconnect | Turn | Now the inverter has neither a source nor a grid connection. Both sides are what makes it isolated rather than just quiet. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Lock both disconnects | Select | Two isolations, two locks. An inverter that gets reset remotely during a string test is a very expensive lesson. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Measure string open-circuit voltage | Graded reading | Voc against the expected value for this string length and temperature is how you find a missing module, a reversed pair or a shaded run before you energise. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Complete the string tests in order | Sequence (strict order: polarity, insulation resistance, earth continuity) | Polarity first, because an insulation test on a reversed string tells you nothing useful and can damage the tester. Continuity last confirms the protective path you have been relying on. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Check the battery state | Graded reading | A battery commissioned at the extremes of its charge window cannot be balanced properly, and the first cycle is when cell imbalance shows itself. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Energise and record | Select | Energise AC then DC, watch the inverter come up, and record the readings. The commissioning record is what the next technician inherits. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `roof-edge` | You walked to an unprotected edge with no anchor. Falls from height are the single largest killer in this trade and the roof does not care how experienced you are. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `hot-mc4` | You pulled an MC4 under load. A live DC string will not stop arcing when the plug separates — the array is a current source for as long as the sun is on it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `battery-terminal` | Those are the battery terminals. A DC battery bank has no zero-crossing: a spanner across them vaporises and takes your hand with it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `damaged-conduit` | That conduit is crushed and the conductors inside are chafed. Energising a damaged run puts fault current into the roof structure. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Line Truck (SmartCiti.X · `line-truck`)

**Category:** Energy & Power  
**Trade:** Outside / overhead lineworker (IBEW)  
**Certification / standard claimed:** IBEW — OSHA 29 CFR 1910.269 qualified electrical worker  
**Tagline:** Bucket-truck line work: rubber goods testing, isolation and correct grounding order  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Hold the tailboard briefing | Select | The minimum approach distance changes with voltage class. Everyone on the crew hears the same number before anyone leaves the ground. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Set the outriggers | Select | A boom worked from an unstabilized truck can shift the whole vehicle under load — the outriggers are what turn the truck into a fixed base. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Bond and ground the truck | Select | If the boom ever contacts an energized conductor, the bond is what keeps the truck body from becoming the fastest path to ground through a person standing on it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Air-test the rubber gloves | Graded reading | A pinhole in rubber goods is invisible until it's under pressure. The air test finds it before the glove ever gets near a conductor. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Inspect the rubber sleeves | Select | Sleeves cover the reach between the glove and the shoulder — a cut there is just as live a path as a cut in the glove itself. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Inspect the bucket liner | Select | The liner is the insulating barrier between you and the boom's conductive structure. A cracked or dirty liner stops being an insulator exactly when you need it to be one. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Dielectric-test the boom | Graded reading | The boom's fibreglass insulation is tested on its own schedule, not assumed sound because it looked fine yesterday. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Confirm the minimum approach distance | Select | The boundary is set before the boom starts moving, not judged by eye once the bucket is already close to the conductor. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Open the line switch | Turn | This is the isolation. Everything from here on is checked against the assumption that a switch position can still be wrong. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Test the line dead with a hot stick | Graded reading | The switch tells you what should be true. The hot stick tester tells you what is actually true on this conductor, right now. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Apply personal protective grounds in order | Sequence (strict order: ground electrode clamp, neutral clamp, phase conductor clamp) | Clamping the ground end first means if the phase turns out to be live when you make that last connection, the low-resistance path is already there waiting for the fault current instead of you. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Teach the line-crawler waypoints | Sequence (strict order: Start waypoint, Mid waypoint, End waypoint) | The crawler drives this span in the order the points were recorded — teach start to end, matching the direction it will actually travel along the conductor. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 13 | Save the crawler path | Select | An untaught point list is just recorded positions. Saving it is what turns three waypoints into a path the crawler can actually run. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 14 | Dry-run the line-crawler path | Timed hold | A brand-new path is verified at a walking pace with a hand on the controller, watching the whole span, before the crawler ever runs it unattended over grounds a crew just applied. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 15 | Confirm rescue readiness | Select | Rescue equipment staged before work starts is the only version that is actually usable in the first thirty seconds of an emergency. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `energized-conductor` | You brought the bucket inside the minimum approach distance without rubber gloves and sleeves donned. Inside that distance the air gap itself is no longer your protection — the rubber is, and it goes on before the boom does. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `damaged-glove` | That rubber glove has a puncture that the air test would have caught. Wearing a failed glove is worse than wearing none, because it tells you it's protecting you right up until it doesn't. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `ground-point-untested` | You reached for the equipment ground before the tester confirmed the line was actually dead. If that conductor is still live, the ground set becomes the path to ground — through whoever is holding it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `boom-control` | That is the boom lift control. Operating the boom before the truck chassis is bonded and grounded leaves the whole vehicle, and anyone touching it, at line potential the instant the boom contacts an energized conductor. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Substation Switching (SmartCiti.X · `substation-switching`)

**Category:** Energy & Power  
**Trade:** Substation electrician / switching operator  
**Certification / standard claimed:** IBEW — utility switching and tagging authorisation; OSHA 29 CFR 1910.269 (electric power generation, transmission and distribution); NFPA 70E arc-flash PPE for the verification  
**Tagline:** Feeder outage on a written switching order: read-back, breaker open, disconnects open, test dead, grounds on, tag, hand-off  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the switching order | Select | A switching order is written by one person and checked by another before it is issued. The operator reads all of it before the first step, because a step makes sense only in the sequence it sits in. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Read back step 1 to the control centre | Select | Two people agree on every switch before it moves. The read-back is the check that the order in your hand is the order the control centre is working from. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Arc-rated PPE for the cubicle | Sequence (any order: arc-rated suit, arc-rated hood and gloves) | The cubicle label gives the incident energy. The PPE matches it, and it is on before anything in the cubicle moves. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Open the feeder breaker | Turn | The breaker is the device rated to interrupt load. It opens first so that everything downstream of it is de-energised before any disconnect moves. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Open the disconnects | Sequence (strict order: line-side disconnect, bus-side disconnect) | With the breaker open there is no current, and the disconnects can move. Two visible breaks isolate the breaker from both sources so it cannot be back-fed from either. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Test the bus dead — every phase | Graded reading | Open disconnects are a claim; the tester is the proof. Every phase, because one back-fed phase is enough. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Apply the grounds | Sequence (strict order: A-phase ground, B-phase ground, C-phase ground) | Grounds make the isolated bus safe to touch: if anything back-feeds, the fault goes to ground, not through the crew. Ground end first so the clamp is never live in your hand. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Torque the ground clamps | Timed hold | A loose ground is a ground that lifts off under fault current, exactly when it is needed. It is torqued, not tightened by feel. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Tag and lock the isolation | Select | The tag names the order, the operator and the crew that now owns the feeder. Nothing on this tag moves until that crew releases it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Walk the cubicle before the hand-off | Find (any order: cracked bus insulator) | The feeder is dead for a window. What is found now gets fixed in the window; what is missed waits for the next outage — or causes it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Report the feeder clear | Select | The hand-off closes the order. The control centre marks the feeder out of service and the maintenance crew holds it until they call it back. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `disconnect-under-load` | You went to open the line disconnect with the breaker still closed. A disconnect is not rated to break load — opening it under 12 kV of feeder current draws an arc across the blades that does not stop until something melts. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `ground-untested` | You reached for the ground clamps before the bus was tested dead. Grounds on an energised bus are a bolted fault with your hands on the lead; the test comes first, every phase, every time. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `skip-readback` | You did the step without reading it back to the control centre. The order exists so two people agree on every switch before it moves — a step done alone is a step the system operator does not know happened. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `no-arc-ppe` | You approached the cubicle for the test without arc-rated PPE. The verification is the one step where the bus might still be live; the label on the cubicle says what to wear, and it is worn. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Battery Yard (SmartCiti.X · `battery-yard`)

**Category:** Energy & Power  
**Trade:** Battery energy storage technician — grid scale  
**Certification / standard claimed:** IBEW outside construction and utility locals; NFPA 855 stationary energy storage installation; NFPA 70E DC arc-flash boundary and shock approach; OSHA 29 CFR 1910.147 lockout/tagout and 1910.269 for the utility interconnection; UL 9540A thermal-runaway test data  
**Tagline:** Grid battery module swap: gas and vent check from outside, stop from the controller, AC before DC, rack disconnects in order, bleed-down wait, live-dead-live on the DC bus, racks grounded, insulated tools, thermal check before the door  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the work order and one-line | Select | A battery yard is a dozen identical enclosures. The one-line says which one is being worked, what the DC bus does when it is opened, and what the technician must be wearing to stand in front of it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Read the enclosure from outside | Sequence (any order: gas detection panel, deflagration vents) | Everything you need to know about whether the enclosure is safe to open is readable from outside it. That is why the panel is mounted outside. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Stop the unit from the controller | Select | A controlled stop ramps the inverter down and opens the contactors in the order the system was designed for. Everything after this is opening something that is already at zero. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Open and lock the AC side | Sequence (strict order: AC breaker open, AC lock and tag) | The AC side is opened first because it is the side that can re-energise from the grid. With it locked, the only energy left in the enclosure is what the cells are holding. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Open the rack DC disconnects | Sequence (strict order: rack 1 disconnect, rack 2 disconnect, rack 3 disconnect) | Each rack is its own string at full bus voltage. They are opened one at a time, in order, so the aisle is never guessing which string is still tied in. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Wait out the bleed-down | Timed hold | The DC link capacitors hold a lethal charge after every disconnect is open. The wait is printed on the door because it is not a judgement call and it cannot be shortened by looking at the meter. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Live, dead, live on the DC bus | Sequence (strict order: meter on the known source, meter on the DC bus, meter on the known source again) | A meter that failed between the first reading and the bus reading will show any bus as dead. Proving it before and after is the only thing that makes 'dead' mean anything. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Confirm the bus is at zero | Graded reading | Zero on this bus means single digits, not 'lower than it was'. Anything above the band means a string is still tied in somewhere and the aisle is still live. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Ground the rack and take insulated tools | Sequence (any order: rack grounding cable, insulated tool set) | The ground holds the rack at zero if a string comes back. The insulated tools are what stop a dropped spanner from becoming a bus bar across two terminals. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Draw the failed module out | Carry and place | A module is sixty kilograms of cells on a rail. It comes out onto a cart, not into someone's arms, and it goes to the quarantine area, not back in the aisle. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Thermal-check the rack | Graded reading | A module fails because something got hot, and the neighbours are the first place that shows. The scan before the door closes is what catches a second bad cell before it becomes the next event. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Walk the enclosure before you close it | Find (any order: suppression bottle out of date) | The enclosure protects itself when nobody is there. Everything that does that is checked before the door closes, because the next person here may be arriving to an alarm. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `open-door-on-alarm` | You opened the enclosure with the gas detector in alarm. A lithium enclosure in off-gas is pre-ignition: opening the door gives the flammable mixture the oxygen it was missing, and the deflagration goes out the door you are standing in. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `dc-before-ac` | You opened the DC rack disconnects with the inverter still running. Breaking DC under load draws an arc that does not self-extinguish the way AC does; the disconnect welds, the arc keeps burning, and the arc-flash boundary is the whole aisle. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `metal-jewellery` | You reached into the rack wearing a ring and a watch. A battery string is a low-voltage, very-high-current source: metal across the terminals does not shock you, it welds to your hand and burns through it in seconds. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `water-on-cells` | You reached for a water extinguisher at a cell fire. Water on a burning lithium module flashes and spreads the event; the enclosure's own suppression and a safe distance are the response, and the fire service is called for the rest. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

# Mobility & Transit

## Signal Cabinet (SmartCiti.X · `signal-cabinet`)

**Category:** Mobility & Transit  
**Trade:** Traffic signal technician  
**Certification / standard claimed:** IBEW — IMSA Level II Traffic Signal Technician certified  
**Tagline:** Intersection work zone, controller fault diagnosis and conflict monitor integrity  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Notify the traffic management centre | Select | The centre is watching this intersection remotely. If they see it drop without warning, they dispatch a second crew into your work zone. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Set the work zone | Sequence (any order: advance warning sign, taper cone, buffer cone, arrow board) | Traffic needs to be told what is happening far enough back to react. The taper is what moves them over; the buffer is what absorbs the one who does not. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | High-visibility clothing | Select | You are a pedestrian in a roadway. Conspicuity is the only protection you have against a driver who is looking at their phone. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Open the controller cabinet | Select | Now that the zone is up and the centre knows, the cabinet can come open — with the door swung to shield you from the lane. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Put the intersection into flash | Select | Flash puts the intersection into a known, fail-safe state that drivers understand. Working a running controller means one wrong card pull changes a phase under a moving car. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Find the cabinet faults | Find (any order: scorched load switch, corroded ground bond, loose detector loop terminal) | Fault-finding is looking, not swapping. Scorching, corrosion and a backed-out conductor each tell you a different part of the story. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Replace the failed load switch | Select | A welded load switch can hold an output on regardless of what the controller commands. It is replaced, never reset. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Test the conflict monitor | Graded reading | The monitor watches for conflicting greens and forces flash. Proving it trips is the whole reason the intersection is allowed back in service. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Set the clearance interval | Graded reading | Clearance timing comes from approach speed and intersection width. Shaving it to improve throughput is how you create the dilemma zone. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Return the intersection to normal | Select | You watch it cycle before you pack up. A controller that comes back with the wrong phase order is your fault until you have seen it run. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Recover the work zone | Select | The zone comes down from the traffic side inward, so you are never the first thing an approaching driver meets unprotected. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `conflict-bypass` | That jumper defeats the conflict monitor. The monitor is the only thing that stops the controller showing green to crossing traffic at the same time. Defeating it is how intersections kill people. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `live-terminals` | You are across the 120 V field terminals with the cabinet live. Those feed the heads outside; the cabinet is powered even in flash. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `step-into-road` | You stepped into the running lane. Your work zone exists so you never have to — stay behind the cones and let traffic pass. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `ladder-unsecured` | That ladder is footed on a kerb, unsecured, beside a live lane. Signal heads get accessed from a properly set ladder or not at all. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Flight Deck (SmartCiti.X · `flight-deck`)

**Category:** Mobility & Transit  
**Trade:** Drone / UAS ground technician  
**Certification / standard claimed:** Teamsters — FAA Part 107 remote pilot certificate holder  
**Tagline:** Vertiport ramp safety: rotor lockout, battery handling, autonomous route planning and pre-flight release  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Check the flight authorization | Select | Airspace and ramp status change by the hour. Working under an aircraft that airspace control expects to be flying is how a ground crew ends up under a live approach. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Set the ground safety zone | Sequence (any order: cone at the ramp edge, cone at the taxi line, ramp barrier) | The zone keeps bystanders off an active ramp and keeps you inside a boundary the flight controller respects for automated landings. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Disarm the flight controller | Select | Disarmed means the motors cannot spin regardless of what a stray signal or a bumped stick commands. Nothing touches the airframe before this. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Fit the rotor locking pins | Select | Disarmed is a software state; the pin is physical. Both together are what makes an accidental spin-up impossible instead of just unlikely. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Complete the pre-flight inspection | Find (any order: chipped propeller, cracked motor arm, obstructed obstacle sensor) | A pre-flight inspection is a search, not a checklist tick. Damage that looks cosmetic on the ramp is structural at altitude. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Replace the damaged propeller | Carry and place | Matched, balanced propellers keep the airframe's vibration profile inside what the flight controller expects. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Inspect and fit the flight battery | Select | The battery bay is the last thing that opens, and only after the airframe cannot spin a motor even if you drop something into it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Verify cell voltage balance | Graded reading | A pack with one cell drifting low will sag under load exactly when the aircraft needs full power to land — balance is checked before every flight, not just at charge. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Contain the damaged battery | Select | A venting cell in a normal storage bin can ignite everything around it. The fireproof bag buys the time for it to be handled properly. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Remove the rotor pins | Select | The pins come out last, right before release — never while there is still a reason for a hand to be near the airframe. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Plan the autonomous delivery route | Sequence (strict order: launch waypoint, relay waypoint, delivery waypoint) | The flight controller flies the waypoints in the order they were entered — swap the relay and the delivery point and the aircraft plans a straight line through restricted airspace instead of around it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Upload the mission | Select | A planned route sitting on the tablet is not a flight plan yet — uploading it is what the aircraft actually flies. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 13 | Verify the route against restricted airspace | Timed hold | Airspace restrictions shift by the hour. You verify the specific route you are about to fly, every time, not the general area it happens to be in. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 14 | Sign the pre-flight release | Select | The release is a signed record that a specific person checked specific things. It is what the incident investigation reads first if something goes wrong in the air. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `spinning-rotor` | You reached toward a rotor with the flight controller still armed. A carbon propeller at flight RPM will not stop for a hand — the aircraft is disarmed and the props are pinned before anyone approaches. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `swollen-battery` | That battery pack is swollen. A puffed LiPo cell is already venting internally; charging or flying it is how a ramp fire starts. It goes straight into the fireproof containment bag. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `ramp-incursion` | You crossed into the active approach path while an aircraft is inbound. The ramp boundary exists because a returning drone is looking for a clear landing pad, not for a person standing on it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `damaged-prop` | That propeller has a chip out of the leading edge. A damaged prop at altitude changes its balance and can shear — it is swapped before the aircraft goes anywhere. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Track Access (SmartCiti.X · `track-access`)

**Category:** Mobility & Transit  
**Trade:** Rail track worker  
**Certification / standard claimed:** BMWED — FRA 49 CFR 214 Roadway Worker Protection qualified  
**Tagline:** Track possession, third-rail isolation and lookout protection  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Attend the possession briefing | Select | Everyone on site works to the same plan. A worker who missed the briefing does not know where the protection actually starts and ends. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Take possession from the signaller | Select | Until the signaller confirms it, the section is still open to traffic as far as the signalling system is concerned. Possession is a spoken confirmation, not an assumption from the timetable. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Isolate the traction current | Select | The isolator is what removes the current, but the switch position alone is not proof — that comes next. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Prove the traction rail is dead | Graded reading | The same rule as any other isolation: measured, not assumed. A tester reading near zero is the only thing that makes the rail approachable. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Mark the limits of possession | Sequence (any order: limit of possession board — up end, limit of possession board — down end) | The boards are the only thing a train crew or another worker can see that marks where your protection actually applies. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Post the lookout | Select | The lookout's only job is watching the line and giving warning in time to clear it. Sighting distance is calculated from line speed, not chosen by convenience. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Fit the rail bonding clamp | Select | The bond maintains the signalling circuit's continuity through the section you are working on, so the system upstream still sees the track correctly. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Measure track gauge | Graded reading | Standard gauge with a small tolerance. Outside it and wheel flanges start climbing the rail instead of following it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Replace the defective fastener | Select | A loose fastener lets the rail move under load. Fixed now, it is a five-minute job; missed, it is a derailment risk. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Complete the tool tally | Select | A tool left on the track is a foreign object under the next train. The tally is counted out and counted back, every time. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Restore traction current | Select | Confirmed clear of the rail, then and only then re-energised. The sequence never reverses. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Hand possession back | Select | The section stays under your protection until you formally hand it back. Walking away without the call is how a possession never actually closes. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `third-rail` | That is the traction current rail. Isolation confirmed elsewhere does not matter here until you have proven this section dead — it carries enough current to kill on contact, no arc required. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `unprotected-crossing` | You crossed the running line without the lookout's clearance. A train at line speed covers that distance faster than you can react to a warning. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `solo-worksite` | You are working without a second person in sight. Track protection is never a one-person job — the lookout exists because you cannot watch the line and do the work at the same time. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `unmarked-boundary` | That limit of possession has no board. Without a marked boundary a train movement has no way to know where your protection actually ends. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Bus Depot Lift (SmartCiti.X · `bus-depot-lift`)

**Category:** Mobility & Transit  
**Trade:** Transit bus technician — electric fleet  
**Certification / standard claimed:** ATU / IAM — ASE Transit Bus (H series) with H8 EV / hybrid-electric; ALI Lifting It Right (ANSI/ALI ALOIM); OSHA 1910.147 lockout for high-voltage disable  
**Tagline:** Electric bus on column lifts: HV disable and prove, chocks, pad placement, synchronised raise, locks down, under-bus work, controlled lower  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the repair order and the lift points | Select | Every model lifts at different frame points and disables HV a different way. The diagram and the procedure are read before anything is touched. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Disable the high-voltage system | Sequence (strict order: master switch, HV service disconnect) | The service disconnect opens the traction battery circuit. It is pulled after the master switch, and it stays in your pocket so no one can reinsert it while you are under the bus. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Prove the HV bus is de-energised | Graded reading | The capacitors hold charge after the disconnect is pulled. The meter, after the wait, is the proof the orange cables are safe. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Chock the wheels | Sequence (any order: front chocks, rear chocks) | The bus must not roll on the pads during the first inches of lift. Chocks at both axles and the brake set before a column moves. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Place the lift pads on the frame points | Carry and place | The frame lift point is engineered to carry the bus; nothing else under there is. The pad goes exactly there, on every column. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Raise in sync | Tracked hold | Four columns raising unevenly twist the frame and shift the load onto two pads. The controller syncs them; the technician watches the level and stops the raise if it drifts. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Lower onto the mechanical locks | Timed hold | The bus is worked under on its locks. Lowering onto them takes the load off the hydraulics, so a hydraulic failure cannot drop it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Work under the bus | Select | Everything before this step is what makes this step ordinary. The motor comes out from under an 18-tonne bus resting on steel. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Inspect under the bus while it is up | Find (any order: chafed HV cable jacket) | A bus is up on locks for an hour a month. What is seen under it now is what keeps it out of the shop next month. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Clear the bay and lower | Select | The lower is called by the person who has looked under the bus. The service disconnect goes back in only when the bus is on the ground and the job is closed. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `under-hydraulic` | You went under the bus with the columns still on hydraulics, locks not engaged. A seal, a hose or a valve is all that is holding 18 tonnes over you; the mechanical locks are what a person works under. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `hv-bare` | You touched the orange high-voltage cable with bare hands before the system was disabled and proven. The traction battery is 600 V and does not care that the bus is switched off. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `pad-on-body` | You put a lift pad on the body skirt instead of the frame lift point. The skirt is sheet metal; it crushes, the bus shifts on the columns, and it comes down where it likes. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `raise-no-chock` | You started the raise with the wheels unchocked and the bus in neutral. A bus that rolls on the pads as it lifts rolls off them. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Airport Ramp (SmartCiti.X · `airport-ramp`)

**Category:** Mobility & Transit  
**Trade:** Airline ramp agent / ground handler  
**Certification / standard claimed:** IAM and Transport Workers Union ramp locals; IATA Ground Operations Manual (AHM 630 ground support equipment, ERA safety envelope); FAA 14 CFR 139.303 personnel training and 139.329 movement-area safety; OSHA 29 CFR 1910.178 for powered ramp equipment  
**Tagline:** Gate turn: FOD walk and equipment restraint line, marshal onto the lead-in, chocks before anything touches it, cones set, ground power and headset to the flight deck, bridge to the door, loader at walking pace, all-clear only when chocks and cones are back  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the turn plan | Select | Stand geometry and aircraft type decide where the chocks and cones go and how far the bridge travels. The turn is planned on the ground before the aircraft is on final. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Walk the stand for FOD | Find (any order: loose bolt on the stand, cargo strap on the lead-in line) | Foreign object damage is the most expensive avoidable thing on a ramp. The walk happens before every arrival, and what is found goes in the bin, not kicked aside. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Stage behind the line | Sequence (any order: equipment restraint line clear, ground equipment behind the line, hi-vis and ear protection) | Until the aircraft is stopped and chocked, the restraint line is the boundary between the ramp crew and a moving aeroplane. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Marshal the aircraft in | Tracked hold | The pilot cannot see the nosewheel or the wingtips. The marshaller's rate and position are the only reference for the last thirty metres. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Stop on the mark | Graded reading | The stop bar is where the bridge reaches the door and the wingtip clears the stand next door. A metre long or short and one of those is no longer true. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Chock and cone | Sequence (strict order: nose gear chocks, main gear chocks, wingtip and engine cones) | Chocks before contact is the rule the whole ramp runs on. The cones mark the parts of the aircraft that ground equipment must never find with a mirror. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Ground power and headset | Sequence (any order: ground power connected, headset to the flight deck) | Ground power lets the engines and the auxiliary unit shut down. The headset is the only direct line to the flight deck for the rest of the turn, and it is what pushback will happen on. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Drive the bridge to the door | Tracked hold | A bridge driven fast into an aircraft door is a hull strike that cancels the next four flights. Steady, watching the sill, with the auto-level following. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Align and dock the bridge | Graded reading | The canopy seals to the fuselage and the auto-level keeps the sill matched as the aircraft rises with unloading. Off-centre, the seal leaks and the sill steps. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Bring the belt loader to the hold | Select | Everything that approaches an aircraft does it at walking pace and stops short. The last metre is done with the platform, not with the vehicle. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Clear the stand and give the all-clear | Sequence (strict order: cones back on the truck, chocks pulled, all-clear to the flight deck) | The chocks come up second to last and the all-clear last, with the chocks visible to the flight deck. That signal is the pilot's proof that nothing is still attached to their aircraft. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `cross-engine-arc` | You walked through the engine's intake arc with the engine turning. A running narrow-body engine ingests a person from several metres in front of it, and the exhaust behind it will blow a baggage cart across the ramp. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `vehicle-past-line` | You drove ground equipment past the equipment-restraint line before the aircraft stopped and was chocked. Every year a piece of ground equipment strikes a moving aircraft on a ramp; the line exists to make that impossible. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `chock-pulled-early` | You pulled the chocks with the bridge still on the aircraft. Nothing holds the aircraft then except its brakes, and a brake release with the bridge attached takes the door off. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `under-fuselage-loading` | You stood under the fuselage while the loader was moving against it. The loader's platform is a shear point against the aircraft skin, and there is no room to get out from under it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

# Water & Environmental

## Valve Vault (SmartCiti.X · `valve-vault`)

**Category:** Water & Environmental  
**Trade:** Water utility operator  
**Certification / standard claimed:** LIUNA — OSHA 29 CFR 1910.146 permit-required confined space entrant  
**Tagline:** Permit-required confined space entry: isolation, atmosphere, roles and retrieval  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Open the entry permit | Select | The permit is the plan. It names the space, who holds each role, how it is isolated and how somebody gets pulled out — before anyone is standing over an open hole. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Guard the opening | Sequence (any order: cone upstream, cone downstream, hatch guard rail) | The opening is a hazard to everyone who is not on your crew. It gets guarded before the cover comes off and stays guarded until it goes back. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Assign the entry roles | Sequence (strict order: entry supervisor, attendant, entrant) | The supervisor signs the permit before anyone is named to a role, and the attendant is posted before the entrant is even suited. Roles come before people move. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Isolate and blank the line | Carry and place | Positive isolation. The blank is a physical plate that cannot be operated from a control room by somebody who does not know you are in there. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Lock the isolation | Select | The lock says the isolation belongs to a person, not to a shift. It comes off when the entry is closed, by the person who put it on. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Start mechanical ventilation | Select | Ventilation runs before and throughout the entry. Where the intake sits matters as much as the fan — clean air in, or you are just circulating the problem. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Test the atmosphere in order | Sequence (strict order: oxygen, flammable gas, toxic gas) | Oxygen comes first because the flammable sensor cannot be trusted in an oxygen-deficient atmosphere. The order is not a convention, it is what makes the other two readings mean anything. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Confirm the oxygen reading | Graded reading | Between 19.5% and 23.5%. Below and you lose consciousness without warning; above and everything in the space becomes far more flammable than it looks. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Rig the retrieval system | Select | A vertical space needs a retrieval line attached before entry, because a rescue that starts by finding equipment has already lost the time it needed. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Fit the full-body harness | Select | The dorsal attachment is what lets a winch lift somebody through an opening they cannot climb out of. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Establish continuous communication | Timed hold | Continuous means continuous. The attendant's only job is to stay in contact and to never, under any circumstance, go in themselves. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Close the permit | Select | The entry is not over when the last person climbs out — it is over when the space is secured and the permit is closed against a headcount. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `rescue-entry` | You went in after the entrant. Unprotected rescuers are the majority of confined space fatalities — the attendant never enters. You call it in and the retrieval line does the work. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `open-hatch-unattended` | You left the opening unguarded. An open vault in a footway takes a pedestrian straight down before anyone can shout. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `petrol-blower` | That is a petrol blower sitting at the opening. Running an engine at the intake pumps carbon monoxide straight into the space you are about to breathe. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `no-blank` | The valve is only closed, not blanked. A closed valve is not an isolation — it can be operated remotely or leak past while somebody is inside the line. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Abatement Chamber (SmartCiti.X · `abatement-chamber`)

**Category:** Water & Environmental  
**Trade:** Asbestos / lead abatement worker  
**Certification / standard claimed:** LIUNA — EPA AHERA-certified asbestos abatement worker  
**Tagline:** Containment integrity, wet-method removal and the three-stage decon airlock  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the containment work plan | Select | The plan sets the containment level and the decon requirements for this specific material — friable asbestos and intact lead paint are not the same job. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Inspect the critical barriers | Select | The poly is the only thing standing between the work area and the rest of the building. A single unsealed seam undoes the whole containment. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Start the negative-air machine | Select | The machine is what actually creates the negative pressure — it runs continuously through the whole job, not just while someone happens to be inside. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Confirm negative pressure on the manometer | Graded reading | Negative pressure is what keeps fibres inside the containment when the flap opens. It is verified on the gauge, never assumed from the machine sounding like it's running. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Fit-check the respirator | Select | A respirator that leaks around the seal protects nobody. The seal is checked every time it goes on, not just the first time it was fitted. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Don PPE in the correct order | Sequence (strict order: coveralls, respirator, taped gloves and boot covers) | The respirator seals against bare skin, not against a hood you put on afterward — and the tape at the end is what keeps a sleeve from riding up mid-task. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Wet the material with amended water | Select | Amended water penetrates the material and keeps fibres bound to it once you start removal — dry material goes airborne the instant it's touched. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Remove the wetted material | Select | Small wetted sections stay wetted through removal. A large dry section outruns how fast you can keep it saturated. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Double-bag and label the waste | Select | The label is what tells everyone downstream — haulers, the landfill, an inspector — exactly what they are handling and how to handle it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Exit through the three-stage decon | Sequence (strict order: dirty room, shower, clean room) | Each stage removes one layer of contamination before the next. Skipping the shower carries contamination straight from the dirty room into clean clothes. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Remove the respirator in the clean room | Select | This is the one place your face is guaranteed to be free of what you were just working around. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Close out the containment log | Select | The log is the record that the containment held pressure and the waste is accounted for — it is what the clearance inspector reads first. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `containment-flap` | You pushed through the containment flap without confirming negative pressure on the manometer. If the enclosure isn't actually under negative pressure, opening it pushes fibres out into the building instead of holding them in. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `dry-removal-tool` | That is a dry scraper. Working the material dry aerosolises fibres immediately — the entire point of the wet method is that a wetted material does not go airborne when it is disturbed. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `early-respirator-removal` | You reached to pull the respirator off before finishing the decon shower. Contamination sits on the outside of that mask and on your skin and hair — it comes off only once you are standing in the clean room. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `unlabeled-bag` | That waste bag is neither double-bagged nor labeled, and it is sitting in the clean side of the airlock. An unlabeled asbestos or lead bag in a clean area is exactly how contamination leaves the site undetected. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Lift Station (SmartCiti.X · `lift-station`)

**Category:** Water & Environmental  
**Trade:** Wastewater collection system operator  
**Certification / standard claimed:** AFSCME / LIUNA — state wastewater collection system operator certification (CWEA Collection System Maintenance Grade II or equivalent); OSHA 29 CFR 1910.146 permit-required confined space; 1910.147 lockout/tagout  
**Tagline:** Wet-well pump pull: gas test, lockout, bypass pumping, guide-rail lift, no-entry retrieval, restart and level check  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the work order and the well data | Select | The order names the pump and the plan names how the flow keeps moving while it is out. A pull without a bypass plan is an overflow with a schedule. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Test the wet-well atmosphere through the vent | Sequence (strict order: oxygen, flammable gas, hydrogen sulfide) | Through the vent, with the hatch closed, so the first reading is taken with no one's face over the opening. Oxygen first because the flammable sensor cannot be trusted without it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Lock out the duty pump | Turn | The level controller will call the pump the moment the well rises. Your lock is what stops it starting while it is hanging on the chain. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Set up bypass pumping | Sequence (strict order: suction hose in the well, discharge to the downstream manhole, bypass pump start) | Suction and discharge are connected before the pump starts, or the start is a spill. The bypass takes the inflow so the well can be worked without rising. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Confirm the bypass is holding the level | Graded reading | A bypass that cannot keep up is not a bypass. The level has to sit below the set point before the duty pump comes out. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Open the hatch and set the guard | Select | The hatch opens onto a drop with H2S at the bottom. The grate keeps a person on the surface while the pump comes through it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Lift the pump on the guide rail | Timed hold | The pump breaks its seal off the discharge elbow and rides the rail to the top. A steady lift; a stalled one leaves it hanging half out of the well. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Land the pump on the pad | Carry and place | The pump is handled on the ground, not in the air. Hands go on it after the chain goes slack. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Find the fault on the pad | Find (any order: ragged impeller) | The pull exists to find this. It is found on the pad, in daylight, with the pump locked out and on the ground. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Rail the pump back, close the hatch, remove the lock, restart | Select | Back in reverse order: the well is closed before the bypass comes off, and the lock comes off last, by the person who put it on. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `well-ladder` | You started down the wet-well ladder. This is a permit-required confined space with hydrogen sulfide in it; the pump comes out on the guide rail from the top, and nobody goes down without a permit, an attendant and retrieval — which is not this job. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `hatch-no-gas` | You opened the hatch before the gas test. The first breath at the opening is the one the meter is for — H2S at the concentration a wet well can hold takes a person down at the hatch, not at the bottom. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `run-no-bypass` | You pulled the duty pump with no bypass running. The inflow does not stop for maintenance; without bypass pumping, the well overflows into the street and the neighbourhood's basements inside the hour. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `chain-under-load` | You reached under the pump while it was hanging on the hoist. Four hundred kilograms on a chain is not a thing you put hands under — it is set down on the pad, then handled. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Chlorine Room (SmartCiti.X · `chlorine-room`)

**Category:** Water & Environmental  
**Trade:** Water treatment plant operator  
**Certification / standard claimed:** State / AWWA Water Treatment Operator Grade II; Chlorine Institute Pamphlets 1 and 65 (cylinder handling, Emergency Kit A); OSHA 29 CFR 1910.1000 chlorine PEL and 1910.134 respiratory protection; NFPA 55 compressed gas storage  
**Tagline:** 150-lb chlorine cylinder change: room monitor and SCBA before the door, empty isolated and capped, full one on the scale and chained, new gasket, yoke a quarter turn past snug, ammonia leak test, valve one turn, wrench on the stem  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the SOP and today's feed rate | Select | The SOP names the setpoint the plant needs and the two-person rule for the room. The change is done the same way every time so the one time it leaks, the response is a habit. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Check the room monitor | Graded reading | The monitor is the only thing that smells chlorine before you do at a concentration that matters. It is read and bump-tested before the door opens, every time. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Stage the SCBA and post the outside operator | Sequence (any order: SCBA at the door, outside operator) | Nobody works a chlorine cylinder alone. The pack is at the door because the leak, if it comes, comes at the yoke where you are standing. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Close the empty cylinder's valve | Turn | The regulator is removed from a closed valve. The cylinder is 'empty' at 20 lb residual and still under vapour pressure. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Remove the regulator, cap and hood the empty | Sequence (strict order: vacuum regulator, outlet cap, valve hood) | The cap seals the outlet and the hood protects the valve stem. An uncapped, unhooded cylinder is the one that snaps a valve when it is rolled. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Bring the full cylinder onto the scale | Carry and place | The scale is how the plant knows what is left: feed is confirmed by weight loss, and an empty is called at 20 lb residual, never run to zero. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Chain the cylinder and fit a new gasket | Sequence (any order: restraint chain, new lead gasket) | A chained cylinder cannot fall on its valve. A new gasket every connection is the Chlorine Institute rule because the old one has already been crushed once. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Tighten the yoke | Graded reading | The lead gasket seals with a quarter turn past snug. Less weeps; more crushes the gasket into the threads and the next change starts with a damaged valve face. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Ammonia leak test every joint | Timed hold | Ammonia vapour meets chlorine and makes a visible white ammonium chloride cloud at a few ppm — a leak you can see before you can smell it. Every joint, every change. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Open the valve one turn | Turn | One turn gives full flow; more turns give nothing but a valve that takes longer to close in a leak. The wrench stays on the stem so the first move in an emergency is already in hand. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Set the feed rate | Graded reading | The setpoint is the residual the plant needs at the tap: too little and the water is not disinfected, too much and the plant gets calls about the taste and the chlorine goes out the vent. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Walk the room before leaving | Find (any order: wrench off the stem) | The room is left the way the next leak needs it: wrench on the stem, kit A sealed and in reach, door closed, monitor live. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `no-scba-entry` | You went into the chlorine room with no SCBA staged and nobody outside. A leak in there is one breath from a hospital; the air pack at the door and the operator watching through the window are what turn a leak into a procedure instead of a rescue. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `reused-gasket` | You reused the old lead gasket. A lead gasket crushes once to seal; the second time it seals until the pressure comes on and then weeps chlorine at the yoke all night. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `water-on-leak` | You put water on a chlorine leak. Chlorine and water make hydrochloric acid — the leak corrodes wider, the cloud grows, and the room becomes an entry for a hazmat team. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `roll-uncapped` | You moved a cylinder with the valve uncapped and unhooded. A snapped valve on a 150-lb cylinder empties it into the room in minutes; the cap and hood go on before the cylinder moves an inch. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Backflow Test (SmartCiti.X · `backflow-test`)

**Category:** Water & Environmental  
**Trade:** Certified backflow prevention assembly tester  
**Certification / standard claimed:** UA plumbers and pipefitters; ASSE 5110 Backflow Prevention Assembly Tester certification and ASSE 1013 reduced-pressure principle assemblies; USC FCCCHR field test procedure; state cross-connection control programme and EPA Safe Drinking Water Act obligations on the purveyor  
**Tagline:** Annual RP assembly test: hazard identified, customer notified before the water goes off, gauge zeroed and hoses bled, check one, check two and the relief opening point each tested in order, assembly restored slowly, report signed and filed  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Identify what the assembly protects against | Select | The degree of hazard decides the assembly. A reduced-pressure assembly is here because what is downstream would be a health hazard in the public main, not merely a nuisance — and that is why this test is not optional. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Tell the customer before anything closes | Sequence (strict order: customer notified, fire protection checked) | The test takes the water off. A building that does not know is a building with no sprinklers, no process water and no warning; a line that feeds fire protection needs the fire service told as well. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Inspect the assembly | Find (any order: relief valve weeping, seized number two test cock) | A continuously weeping relief is a failing assembly reporting itself. Finding it before the test means the repair is planned rather than discovered halfway through with the water off. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Zero the gauge and bleed the hoses | Timed hold | Air in a hose compresses and makes every differential read low. A bled gauge is the difference between a test and a guess, and the guess goes on a certificate with your number on it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Close the downstream shutoff | Turn | The test measures the assembly, not the building. Downstream demand while a check is being tested pulls the reading around and hides a failing check. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Test check valve number one | Graded reading | The first check is the primary barrier. It has to hold a minimum differential above the relief opening point, or the relief will be doing the first check's job every time the pressure moves. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Test check valve number two | Graded reading | The second check is the backup that holds while the relief dumps. It is tested separately because an assembly can pass on one check and be one failure away from a cross connection. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Test the relief valve opening point | Graded reading | The relief opening point is what dumps the zone to atmosphere before backflow can happen. It has to open above a set differential and below the first check's holding pressure, or the assembly has no safe window. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Close the test cocks in order | Sequence (strict order: number four test cock, number three, number two, number one) | Closing in reverse order keeps the kit from trapping pressure and keeps the assembly's zone from being back-fed through the test equipment as it comes off. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Restore the service slowly | Tracked hold | A slow restore lets the building's pipework fill without a hammer. Thrown open, the surge breaks joints, blows out fixture supplies and can damage the assembly that was just certified. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Record, sign and file the report | Sequence (strict order: readings recorded, report signed with the certification number, copy filed with the water purveyor) | The purveyor's file is what makes the programme real: an assembly with no filed test is treated as failed, and the tester's number on the report is what makes the reading accountable to somebody. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `bypass-the-assembly` | You opened the bypass around the assembly to keep the building in water. For as long as that bypass is open the building's plumbing is connected straight to the public main with nothing between them — which is the exact cross connection the assembly exists to prevent. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `no-notice` | You shut the water off with no notice to the customer. A building loses its fire sprinklers, its dialysis chairs or its production line the moment that valve closes, and the first anyone knows is when something fails. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `unbled-gauge` | You tested on a gauge with air in the hoses. Air compresses and water does not, so every reading is low by an unknown amount, and an assembly that actually failed gets a passing report with your certification number on it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `fast-restore` | You threw the shutoff open to restore the service. A sudden restore slams a water hammer through the building's pipework, which breaks joints, fixtures and sometimes the assembly you just certified. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

# Connectivity & Telecom

## Splice Node (SmartCiti.X · `splice-node`)

**Category:** Connectivity & Telecom  
**Trade:** Fibre optic technician  
**Certification / standard claimed:** CWA — BICSI Installer 2, Optical Fiber Technician certified  
**Tagline:** Dark-fibre confirmation, laser safety, cleave quality and splice loss budget  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the ticket and circuit ID | Select | One fibre in that tray carries a hospital's imaging link. The ticket is what tells you which strand is yours and when you are allowed to break it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Shut down the transmitter | Select | You make the fibre dark at the source. Assuming it is dark because the ticket says so is how technicians meet a live 1550 nm carrier. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Prove the fibre is dark | Graded reading | Dark is a measurement in dBm, not a belief. Anything above the safe threshold means something is still transmitting into that strand. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Put on the laser eyewear | Select | Rated for the wavelengths in this node, not generic safety glasses. They are the backstop for the port somebody else brings back up while you are working. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Open the splice tray | Select | Trays are opened gently and one at a time. Every fibre in there is somebody's service, and macro-bends you introduce today become faults next month. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Strip the buffer coating | Select | A clean strip with no nicks. A scratched cladding will pass a visual check and then break in the tray under thermal cycling. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Clean the bare fibre | Select | Contamination is the single largest cause of high-loss splices. The cleave and the arc both assume a clean surface. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Cleave to angle | Graded reading | The end face has to be perpendicular. Half a degree of angle scatters light at the joint and no amount of arc power recovers it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Fusion splice the joint | Select | The splicer aligns the cores and fuses them. What you control is everything that happened before this button. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Verify the splice loss | Graded reading | The link has a loss budget and every joint spends part of it. A splice outside budget gets cut out and redone now, not explained later. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Shrink the protection sleeve | Select | The sleeve is the joint's only mechanical strength. An unprotected fusion splice survives the bench and fails in the tray. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Label and document | Select | The record is what the next technician trusts at 3 am. An unlabelled tray costs somebody an outage window to re-identify. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `bare-fibre-end` | You looked into the fibre end. Transmission wavelengths are invisible and your blink reflex never fires — the retinal burn is done before you know the port was live. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `fibre-scraps` | Those are cleaved fibre offcuts on the bench. Bare glass shards work into skin and cannot be seen on an X-ray. They go in the sharps tube, immediately, every time. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `alcohol-heat` | Isopropyl beside a running heat oven. The cleaning solvent is flammable and the oven cycles to 200 °C — one goes away before the other is switched on. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `unlabelled-port` | That port has no label and no lockout. Working an unidentified port means the service you take down belongs to somebody who did not know it was coming. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Tower Climb (SmartCiti.X · `tower-climb`)

**Category:** Connectivity & Telecom  
**Trade:** Telecom / broadcast tower technician  
**Certification / standard claimed:** CWA — NATE Tower Climber Level II certified  
**Tagline:** Guyed tower climb: 100% tie-off, RF lockout and a controlled descent  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the climb permit | Select | The permit names every transmitter that can reach this structure. You plan the RF survey and the climb route from it before a single boot touches the first rung. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Survey the RF field at the work height | Graded reading | RF exposure is invisible and cumulative. A survey at the actual work height, not the ground, is what tells you whether the antennas nearby need to come off air before you go up. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Lock out the transmitters | Select | A verbal 'it's off' is not lockout. The physical lock is what stops someone downstairs re-energising the feed while you are standing next to the radiating element. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Brief the rescue plan | Select | A suspension trauma rescue has to start inside minutes. The plan is worthless if the first time the climb team hears it is after someone is already hanging in a harness. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Don the fall-arrest gear | Sequence (strict order: full-body harness, lanyard 1, lanyard 2) | The harness goes on before either lanyard has anything to clip to. Two lanyards is what makes 100% tie-off possible — one stays anchored while the other moves to the next point. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Tether every tool | Select | Anything not tethered is a dropped-object hazard the moment your hand opens for any reason. Tethering happens at the ground, not once you notice a tool is loose at height. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Walk the structure inspection | Find (any order: frayed guy wire strand, cracked step bolt, corroded guy anchor) | A structural inspection is a search before every climb, not an assumption that yesterday's tower is today's tower. Weather and corrosion do not wait for the annual inspection. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Climb, maintaining 100% tie-off | Sequence (strict order: lower climb anchor, mid climb anchor, upper climb anchor) | One lanyard stays connected while the other reaches for the next anchor. That overlap is the entire concept of 100% tie-off — it only works taken in order, low to high. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Replace the antenna mounting bracket | Select | The actual repair only happens once every control ahead of it is already in place — locked-out RF, inspected structure, tied-off climber. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Recover and stow tools | Select | A tool count at height matches the ground crew's tool count before you climbed. Anything unaccounted for stays a dropped-object risk until it is found. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Make a controlled descent | Timed hold | A controlled descent is a held skill, not a release. Letting go partway to 'save time' is how a controlled descent becomes an uncontrolled one. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Clear the RF lockout | Select | The lock does not come off until every climber has confirmed clear. Restoring power to an antenna with someone still on the tower is exactly what the lockout existed to prevent. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `unclipped-move` | That beam is past your last clipped point. Moving to it before the second lanyard is already engaged leaves you connected to nothing during the transfer, and a transfer is exactly when climbers slip. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `live-antenna-panel` | That antenna panel is still transmitting. Working this close without RF lockout exposes you to RF heating and lets stray current arc into an ungrounded tool the instant it touches the feed line. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `loose-tool` | That wrench is sitting untethered on the platform grating. A dropped tool from this height hits the ground crew below with the force of a weight dropped from a moving vehicle, not a hand tool. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `frayed-lanyard-hazard` | That lanyard's webbing is frayed clean through the outer sheath. A shock-absorbing lanyard is only rated for one fall event, and damaged webbing can part well before it ever reaches that rating. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Cell Site Battery (SmartCiti.X · `cell-site-battery`)

**Category:** Connectivity & Telecom  
**Trade:** Cell site / DC power technician  
**Certification / standard claimed:** CWA — telecom DC power plant (−48 V) technician; OSHA 29 CFR 1910.305(j)(7) / IEEE 450 stationary battery maintenance; NFPA 70E for the electrical work  
**Tagline:** −48 V plant string replacement: ventilate for hydrogen, PPE, isolate the string, insulated tools, terminal covers, cell checks, return to bus  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the method of procedure | Select | The MOP names the string, the isolation point and the numbers. On a live plant, working from memory is how the wrong string gets opened. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Ventilate the cabinet and check for hydrogen | Select | Charging batteries off-gas hydrogen. A closed cabinet can hold enough to ignite from a tool spark; ventilated and read below the limit is the precondition for everything else. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Face shield, insulated gloves, jewellery off | Sequence (any order: face shield, insulated gloves, jewellery off) | A short on a battery is a flash and a spray of electrolyte. The shield and gloves are for that; the ring goes in the pocket because it is the short. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Isolate the failed string | Turn | The string comes off the bus at its breaker so the work is on an isolated string, not a live plant. The site stays up on the other string. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Verify the string is off the bus | Graded reading | An open breaker is a claim; the meter is the proof. The reading says whether the terminals you are about to touch are on the bus or off it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Cover the adjacent terminals | Sequence (any order: terminal cover 1, terminal cover 2, terminal cover 3) | The wrench only needs to touch two posts. Covering every post but the one in hand is how a slip stays a slip. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Fit the replacement battery | Carry and place | The failed cell comes out, the new one goes in the same position, same polarity, same orientation. Checked twice before the intercell links go on. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Torque the intercell links | Timed hold | Under-torqued links heat under load; over-torqued ones crack the post. The figure is the manufacturer's, the wrench is insulated, and the other hand is nowhere near a terminal. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Check the string cell by cell | Graded reading | One low cell drags a string; one high cell cooks. Each cell reads inside the float band before the string is trusted on the bus. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Return the string to the bus | Select | Back on the bus, the new string floats with the old. Load sharing is checked at the plant controller before the cabinet closes. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Walk the plant before closing up | Find (any order: corroded post on string 2) | The site is up on the other string. Whether it stays up on the next outage depends on what you noticed on the way out. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `bare-wrench` | You picked up the uninsulated wrench. Across two battery terminals it is a dead short with hundreds of amps behind it — it welds itself to the posts and sprays molten metal at your face. Insulated tools, and one hand at a time. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `ring-on` | You went at the terminals with your ring on. Metal jewellery across a terminal is a short that does not come off; it goes in your pocket before the cabinet opens. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `no-vent-spark` | You used the cordless drill inside the unventilated cabinet. Charging batteries make hydrogen; the cabinet is ventilated and checked before a tool that can spark goes inside it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `wrong-string` | You started on string 2 — the live string still feeding the bus. The failed string is isolated at its breaker and identified before a terminal is touched; the other string is what keeps the site up. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
