## Microwave Backhaul (SmartCiti.X · `microwave-backhaul`)

**Category:** Connectivity & Telecom  
**Trade:** Microwave / RF technician — backhaul  
**Certification / standard claimed:** CWA and IBEW telecom locals; FCC 47 CFR 1.1310 maximum permissible exposure and OSHA 29 CFR 1910.268(p) RF work practices; NATE CTS tower/rooftop climber; TIA-222 structural loading and TIA-1019 rigging  
**Tagline:** Rooftop link re-alignment: RF survey and MPE boundary, far end keyed down and locked out, fall protection before the parapet, dish freed, panned and tilted on receive level, polarisation proven, torqued, weatherproofed, path handed back  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the RF site survey | Select | A rooftop carries other people's antennas. The survey says which are transmitting, where the boundary is and who has to be called before you cross it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Find the MPE boundary | Select | The boundary is where the field exceeds the exposure limit for an occupational worker. It is marked so the crew knows what 'stand clear' means in metres, not in feel. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Call the far end | Select | The other end of a link is a radio somewhere else that nobody on this roof controls. It comes down by agreement, logged, before a hand touches the dish. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Key down and lock out the transmitter | Sequence (strict order: transmitter keyed down, transmit breaker open, lock and tag) | Keyed down is a software state and software states come back. The breaker and the lock are what stop the radio re-keying on a timer or a remote hand while you are in front of the dish. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Set up for the parapet | Sequence (any order: roof anchor, harness and lanyard, tools tethered) | The dish is at the edge. The anchor holds the technician, the tether holds everything else, and the pavement below is a public sidewalk. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Inspect the mount and radome | Find (any order: cracked mount weld, torn radome cover) | A link that drifted did not drift on its own. The damage is found and recorded before the alignment, or the alignment walks off again in the next wind. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Free the azimuth clamp | Turn | The clamp holds the dish against wind load. It is loosened, not removed, so the dish is always held enough to stop it running away when the wind takes the face. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Pan onto the far site | Tracked hold | A backhaul beam is a couple of degrees wide. Sweeping slowly is the only way to find the main lobe instead of locking onto a side lobe that will drop the link in the first rain. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Set the elevation | Graded reading | Elevation is the other half of the beam. The peak is a single point in two axes, and the number on the meter is the only honest way to find it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Confirm the receive level | Graded reading | The design receive level is what the path was engineered for. A link three decibels low works on a clear day and fails in the rain fade it was built to survive. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Torque, weatherproof and hand back | Sequence (strict order: clamps torqued, connectors weatherproofed, path handed back) | The alignment lasts exactly as long as the clamp torque and the weatherproofing. The hand-back is what tells the network the link is carrying traffic again. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `in-beam-live` | You stepped in front of a live microwave feed. At the dish face a backhaul link is far above the maximum permissible exposure limit; the injury is thermal, it is to the eyes and skin, and there is nothing to feel until the damage is done. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `parapet-unanchored` | You worked at the parapet with no anchor. A rooftop edge is a fall hazard at any height, and a dish that swings while you are leaning on it takes your balance with it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `climb-with-tools-loose` | You went up with loose tools and hardware in an open pouch. Anything dropped from a roof is lethal on the pavement below; tools are tethered and hardware is in a closed container before the ladder. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `hot-swap-waveguide` | You opened the waveguide flange with the transmitter still keyed. Opening a pressurised, energised waveguide sprays the dry air charge and puts the full transmit power out of an open flange at head height. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

# Emergency Services

## Triage Point (SmartCiti.X · `triage-point`)

**Category:** Emergency Services  
**Trade:** EMT / paramedic  
**Certification / standard claimed:** IAEP — NREMT-Paramedic certified  
**Tagline:** START mass-casualty triage: rapid assessment, tagging and the golden hour  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Scene size-up | Select | You triage differently for four casualties than for forty. The size-up is what calibrates your pace before you touch anyone. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Glove and mask before contact | Select | Universal precautions apply to every casualty at every scene. This is the one thing you do before the clock on triage even starts. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Sweep and assess every casualty | Find (any order: immediate casualty, delayed casualty, walking wounded, deceased / expectant casualty) | START triage is a single, fast pass through everyone before you commit to treating anyone. The whole method exists to answer 'who needs me most' before you run out of time to ask. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Open the airway and reassess | Timed hold | A casualty who is not breathing until the airway is opened is not the same as one who is not breathing at all. The full assessment window is what tells them apart. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Tag immediate | Select | Red means treat now, transport first. This casualty gets the next available resource the moment one exists. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Tag delayed | Select | Stable enough to wait, not stable enough to walk. Yellow is reassessed continuously in case their status changes. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Tag minor | Select | Green still needs tracking. An uncounted casualty who walks off is one the scene commander has to account for later, usually at the worst time. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Tag deceased / expectant | Select | This tag is only applied after the airway manoeuvre, not on first sight. It redirects effort to casualties who can still be saved with the resources on scene. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Shore the unstable debris | Select | The rescuer becomes a second casualty the moment the thing that caused the first collapse moves again. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Hand off to transport | Select | Triage only works if the count reaches whoever is deciding transport order. A tag with nobody told about it is a casualty who waits for the next crew to notice them. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `unstable-debris` | That beam is still shifting. Treating a casualty under unstable debris without shoring it first means the next collapse takes the rescuer too — scene safety comes before patient contact. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `walk-past-green` | You walked past a casualty who can walk without tagging them. Green casualties still need a tag — an untagged 'walking wounded' is a casualty who disappears from the count and shows up later as a surprise. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `cpr-on-black` | You are starting CPR on a black-tagged casualty. In a mass-casualty START protocol, resources go to the casualties who can be saved with the time you have — sustained resuscitation on the deceased-expectant category costs the salvageable ones their golden hour. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `no-gloves` | You made patient contact with no gloves. Every casualty at a mass-casualty scene is an unknown bloodborne exposure risk until proven otherwise. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Decon Line (SmartCiti.X · `decon-line`)

**Category:** Emergency Services  
**Trade:** Hazmat firefighter / decon technician  
**Certification / standard claimed:** IAFF — NFPA 470 hazardous materials operations (decontamination mission-specific competency); OSHA 29 CFR 1910.120(q) emergency response  
**Tagline:** Hazmat decontamination corridor: zones by the wind, pools in order, gross wash, doffing order, runoff contained  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the incident action plan | Select | The plan says what the product is, whether it is a water-soluble wash or a dry decon, and where the zones go. The corridor is built from the plan, not from habit. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Check the wind | Select | Decon is set upwind and uphill of the release so the crew working the line is not in the plume. The flag decides the direction of every zone. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Establish the zones | Sequence (strict order: hot zone line, warm zone line, cold zone line) | Hot first because it bounds the hazard; warm is the corridor; cold is where clean people stand. Setting cold first puts the safe area before anyone knows where the danger ends. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Lay out the corridor | Sequence (strict order: gross decon pool, technical wash pool, rinse pool) | Each pool takes off what the last left. Gross removes the bulk, technical scrubs the suit, rinse clears the soap — reversed, the responder arrives at cold still carrying product. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Suit the decon crew | Sequence (any order: splash suit, outer gloves, SCBA) | The decon crew is inside the warm zone. Everything they wash off a responder is in the air and on the ground around them; they are protected to the same level, minus one, as the entry team. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Berm the drain | Carry and place | Runoff is the product in water. The drain is bermed before the first litre is sprayed, and the pool liners drain to the collection tank. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Charge the decon line | Turn | Low pressure, high volume: a wash that rinses without driving product through the suit fabric. The line charges once there is somewhere for the water to go. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Gross-decon the responder | Tracked hold | Top down, so what runs off runs off clean skin of the suit below. Steady pressure: enough to move product, not enough to drive it into seams. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Doff in order | Sequence (strict order: outer boots and gloves, suit, SCBA and mask) | The mask stays on until the suit is off because the suit's outside is still the release. Air on the face comes last, in clean air. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Medical monitoring | Graded reading | Heat, exertion and the product all show in the numbers. A responder is not released from decon until medical says the vitals are inside criteria. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Walk the corridor for leaks | Find (any order: torn pool liner) | A corridor that looks right can still be leaking. The walk-down is what proves not a litre left the line. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `shortcut-hot` | You stepped from the hot zone straight into the cold zone. Every step of the corridor exists to leave contamination behind; a shortcut carries it into the rehab area, the ambulance and the crew that never suited up. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `mask-off-warm` | You pulled the mask off in the warm zone. The SCBA comes off last, in the cold zone, after the suit — the air around the corridor is still the release's air. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `runoff-drain` | You let the wash water run to the storm drain. Decon runoff is the contaminant in solution; it is bermed and pumped to the collection tank, never sent downstream. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `bare-hands-rinse` | You rinsed a responder with bare hands. The decon crew is in PPE too — the corridor's own people are exposed to everything they wash off. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Aerial Ladder (SmartCiti.X · `aerial-ladder`)

**Category:** Emergency Services  
**Trade:** Firefighter — aerial apparatus driver/operator  
**Certification / standard claimed:** IAFF — NFPA 1002 Chapter 6 aerial apparatus driver/operator; NFPA 1901 aerial device stabilization and operating limits; NFPA 1500 / OSHA 1910.269 ten-foot clearance from energised overhead lines  
**Tagline:** Aerial apparatus set-up: size-up, spot at the corner outside the collapse zone, chock, stabilizers on pads to level, PTO, raise and rotate clear of the lines, tip above the roofline, lock, belt, report  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the size-up | Select | The spot is decided before the truck stops rolling: where the lines are, how tall the wall is, which way the smoke goes. Everything after this step is placed against those four facts. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Identify the energised lines | Select | The lines are the one thing on this fireground that does not give a warning. Naming them first means every rotation and extension is planned around them. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Spot the apparatus | Select | The corner gives the aerial two sides to work and puts the turntable outside the arc a failing wall covers. Closer looks faster and is the collapse zone. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Chock the wheels | Sequence (any order: front wheel chock, rear wheel chock) | The parking brake holds a parked truck; it does not hold a truck the stabilizers are about to lift and push sideways. Chocks first, every time. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Pad the soft ground | Carry and place | A stabilizer puts tons through a plate the size of a dinner tray. Soil, asphalt on a hot day or a grate needs a pad to spread it, or the truck settles on that side under load. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Set the stabilizers | Sequence (any order: left stabilizer, right stabilizer) | Full extension is the rated footprint. A short-jacked side has a fraction of the load chart and no warning until the aerial swings over it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Level the turntable | Graded reading | The load chart assumes a level turntable. Every degree off level is side-load on the ladder sections and a tip that lands somewhere other than where it was aimed. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Engage the PTO | Turn | The PTO takes engine power to the aerial hydraulics. It is engaged only when the base is set, because from this point the controls move the ladder. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Raise and rotate clear of the lines | Tracked hold | Smooth is safe: the ladder is a long lever and a jerk at the pedestal is a whip at the tip. The rotation goes the long way round if the short way passes the lines. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Extend the tip to the roofline | Graded reading | The tip lands a couple of feet above the roofline so a firefighter steps off level, and the ladder is not resting its load on the parapet or reaching past where anyone can use it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Lock, belt, report | Sequence (strict order: ladder locks, ladder belt, tip report) | Locks so the sections cannot creep under a climber; the belt before the first rung; the report so command knows the roof has a way off. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Walk the set-up under load | Find (any order: stabilizer foot lifting) | The set-up is proven under load, not at rest. A foot that has lifted means the truck is standing on three points and the load chart no longer applies. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `spot-collapse-zone` | You spotted the apparatus inside the collapse zone. A wall falls one and a half times its height outward; the truck, the turntable and the crew on it are inside that arc. Spot at the corner, outside the zone, and let the aerial reach in. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `lines-contact` | You swung the aerial inside ten feet of the energised lines. An aerial ladder is a grounded steel conductor with a firefighter on it; contact or arc-over at that distance kills the operator at the pedestal and the crew at the tip. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `climb-unbelted` | You climbed the aerial without clipping the ladder belt. The tip moves with the building, the wind and the stream; a belt is what keeps a firefighter on a ladder that is sixty feet in the air. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `no-pad-soft` | You set a stabilizer down on soft ground with no pad. The ground gives under the point load, the truck tilts, and the aerial's rated load drops to nothing on the low side. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Confined Rescue (SmartCiti.X · `confined-rescue`)

**Category:** Emergency Services  
**Trade:** Technical rescue technician — confined space  
**Certification / standard claimed:** IAFF — NFPA 1006 confined-space rescue technician and NFPA 1670 operations level; OSHA 29 CFR 1910.146(k) permit-space rescue and 1910.134(g)(3) two-in two-out; ANSI Z359 fall-arrest and rescue systems  
**Tagline:** Below-grade rescue: refuse the unprotected entry, isolate and ventilate, monitor top to bottom, tripod and mechanical advantage rigged, air-supplied entrant on a tended line, patient packaged, controlled haul, atmosphere log handed over  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Size up the space | Select | The first decision is whether this is a rescue or a recovery, and whether anyone is going in at all. The permit and the space tell you both before a rope comes off the truck. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Hold the crew at the hole | Select | The instinct is to go in. The job of the first rescuer on scene is to make sure the second victim is not one of their own, and that means physically holding the line at the opening. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Isolate the space | Sequence (strict order: feed valve closed, lock and tag, blank flange in) | A closed valve is a valve somebody can open. The lock stops the hand and the blank stops the valve; both, because the entrant will be under that line for twenty minutes. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Set the ventilation | Select | Ventilation moves air at the bottom where the heavy gas sits, and it has to draw from air that is actually clean. Where the intake sits is most of whether the fan helps at all. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Monitor top, middle and bottom | Graded reading | Gases stratify: oxygen at the top says nothing about the bottom. The meter goes down in stages, and the entry decision is made on the worst reading, not the first. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Rig the system | Sequence (strict order: tripod set and legs chained, main line and mechanical advantage, belay line on a separate anchor) | Two lines on two anchors, because the whole system has to survive losing any one part of it while a person is hanging on it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Prepare the entrant | Sequence (any order: entrant on supplied air, attendant at the opening, comms to the entrant) | The attendant never leaves the hole and never enters. Their whole job is the log, the line and the count of who is in. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Lower the entrant | Tracked hold | A controlled lower keeps the entrant off the walls and the ladder, and keeps the belay in step. Speed here buys nothing and costs control. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Package the patient | Sequence (strict order: airway and air on the patient, rescue harness fitted, patient clipped to the main line) | Air before movement: the patient has been breathing whatever put them down. The harness distributes the lift, and the clip goes on last so nothing is under tension while it is being fitted. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Haul the patient out | Tracked hold | A steady haul lets the attendant watch the patient clear the opening and the belay stay tight. Jerking a haul line with a person on it is what breaks an edge roller or a shoulder. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Hand over and secure | Sequence (strict order: patient to EMS, atmosphere log handed over, space secured and tagged) | The hospital treats what the meter recorded, not what the space looked like. The log goes with the patient, and the space stays closed until the employer's investigation is done. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `rush-in-unprotected` | You went in after the patient without air or a line. More than half of the people who die in confined spaces are the would-be rescuers; the atmosphere that put the worker down is still there, and now there are two patients and nobody at the hole. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `no-isolation` | You started the rescue with the line into the chamber still live. Whatever filled that space is still being fed into it, and it will fill again behind the entrant and the fan. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `vent-into-space` | You pointed the blower so it pulled from beside the exhaust. Ventilating with the discharge of an engine or the plume you just pushed out is how a space gets worse while the meter says the fan is running. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `haul-no-belay` | You hauled on the main line with no belay. A single-line haul with a person on it is one jammed pulley or one cut sheath from a drop back into the space with the patient still attached. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

# Manufacturing & Automation

## Robot Cell (SmartCiti.X · `robot-cell`)

**Category:** Manufacturing & Automation  
**Trade:** Automation / robotics technician  
**Certification / standard claimed:** UAW — ANSI/RIA R15.06 robot safety qualified  
**Tagline:** Six-axis robot cell lockout, light-curtain integrity and teach-pendant pick-and-place programming  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the maintenance work order | Select | Cells share safety zones on some lines. The work order tells you which arm is yours and which isolation actually covers it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Hit the cell emergency stop | Select | E-stop takes the program out of automatic immediately. It is the first move, and it buys you the moment you need to isolate properly. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Open the main disconnect | Turn | The e-stop is a control-level stop; the disconnect is the isolation. A cell that only e-stopped can still be reset from the HMI by someone who does not know you are inside. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Lock the disconnect | Select | Your lock says the isolation belongs to you specifically. Nobody re-energises a cell with your lock still on it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Bleed the pneumatic system | Select | Stored compressed air moves an actuator with the electrics dead. Zero energy means every energy source, not just the one with a switch. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Verify zero energy at the controller | Graded reading | The disconnect being open is not proof by itself — a fault or a miswired feed can leave a path live. You confirm it the same way for every isolation. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Open the cell gate | Select | The gate interlock exists to stop this exact step happening before isolation. With power confirmed off, it is finally the right moment. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Service the end effector | Select | With the arm at zero energy and the air bled, the gripper is finally just a mechanism, not a hazard. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Restore power and unlock | Select | Your lock, your call — nobody removes it but you, and you confirm you are the last one out before it comes off. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Jog the arm in teach mode | Graded reading | Teach mode is deliberately capped well below production speed, because a person is physically present at the pendant with a dead-man switch, not behind a guard. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Teach the pick-and-place waypoints | Sequence (strict order: Pick point, Transfer point, Place point) | A taught path is only as safe as the order it was recorded in — teach the transfer point after place and the arm swings back through the fence line on playback instead of clearing it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Save the taught program | Select | An untaught point list is just jog history. Saving it is what turns three recorded positions into a program the controller can actually execute. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 13 | Dry-run the program at teach speed | Timed hold | You verify a brand-new program at teach speed with your hand on the pendant's dead-man switch, watching every point, before it ever runs unattended at full production speed. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 14 | Close and verify the guard | Select | A gate that looks closed but has not re-engaged the interlock will run the cell in automatic with the guard effectively open. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `curtain-bypass` | That jumper defeats the light curtain. The curtain is the only thing that stops the arm the instant a body crosses the plane — bypassing it to 'just check something quickly' is exactly how reach-in injuries happen. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `arm-envelope` | You stepped inside the robot's reach envelope with the controller still in automatic. The arm does not know you are there and it does not slow down for you. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `pinch-point` | That is the gripper's closing pinch point. Even in manual jog mode a six-axis end effector closes with enough force to crush a hand. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `residual-air` | That pneumatic line is still charged. Electrical lockout does not bleed stored air — a charged actuator can still fire the gripper with the power off. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Press Brake (SmartCiti.X · `press-brake`)

**Category:** Manufacturing & Automation  
**Trade:** Sheet-metal press brake operator  
**Certification / standard claimed:** IAM — OSHA 29 CFR 1910.147 lockout/tagout; ANSI B11.3 press brake safeguarding; light-curtain (presence-sensing device) verification per manufacturer's procedure  
**Tagline:** Tooling change under lockout, light-curtain proof, back gauge, tonnage, first-article bend  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the job traveler | Select | The traveler names the V-die, the punch, the tonnage and the angle. A setup starts from the drawing, not from whatever tooling is already in the machine. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Lock out control power | Turn | The ram is hydraulic; a pedal bump or a control fault during a tooling change closes it. Your lock is what makes the die space a safe place for hands. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Install and seat the V-die | Carry and place | The die goes in square, seated against the holder, then clamped. A die that is not seated bends a part wrong at best and ejects at worst. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Clamp the tooling | Select | Clamped tooling stays where it was set through every stroke. Unclamped tooling is the one thing the press can throw at you. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Prove the light curtain | Sequence (strict order: top beam, middle beam, lowest beam) | A curtain is proven, not assumed. Every beam is tested every setup because a single failed beam at hand height is a curtain that does not exist where it matters. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Set the back gauge | Graded reading | The gauge sets the flange length. It is set to the drawing and then proven on a first article — never eyeballed against the last job's part. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Set the tonnage | Graded reading | Too little and the bend is short; too much and you crack the die or the punch. The chart number is the number. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Bend a first article on scrap | Select | The first stroke is a test, on a piece you can throw away. It proves the setup before it proves it on the customer's part. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Check the bend angle | Graded reading | Springback means the ram depth and the angle are not the same number. The protractor is what says the setup is right; the drawing's tolerance is the band. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Take the production stroke | Timed hold | A stroke released halfway leaves the part pinched and the ram mid-travel. Hands placed first, then a full, deliberate stroke. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Walk the machine before the run | Find (any order: gap in the side guard) | The curtain guards the front. The sides and back are fixed guards, and a missing one is a route into the die space nobody is watching. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `reach-in` | You reached into the die space with control power on. The pedal is inches from your foot and the ram does not care what your hands are doing — tooling is touched with the machine locked out, never live. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `curtain-bypass` | You turned the curtain bypass key. The presence-sensing device is the safeguard for every stroke; muting it to save a second turns a press brake into a machine with no guard at all. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `unclamped-die` | You went to bend on an unclamped die. Tooling that is not seated and clamped can eject under load — sideways, at the operator, at tonnes of force. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `part-whip` | You held the part flat with your hands beside the die line. On the upstroke a long part whips up faster than you can move; hands go behind the flange, on the supports, never in the arc. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Conveyor Guard (SmartCiti.X · `conveyor-guard`)

**Category:** Manufacturing & Automation  
**Trade:** Conveyor maintenance technician  
**Certification / standard claimed:** UAW / IAM — OSHA 29 CFR 1910.147 lockout/tagout; ASME B20.1 conveyor safety (guarding of nip points, emergency stops); 1910.212 machine guarding  
**Tagline:** Belt jam clearance: e-stop, lockout, stored-energy release, try-start, guard off and back on, pull-cord restored, restart  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the ticket | Select | One conveyor can have a drive, a take-up and a gravity section, each with its own energy. The ticket's lockout points are the list; you do not discover them at the pulley. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Hit the e-stop | Select | The e-stop is the immediate stop. It is not a lockout — it is what buys the seconds to get to one. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Lock out the drive | Turn | A stopped belt restarts the moment the jam clears and the controller sees the sensor go clear. Your lock is what stops that. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Release the stored energy | Timed hold | A locked-out belt still has tension in it from the take-up weight. Cut a jam free under tension and the belt snaps taut — with your hand in it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Try-start | Select | The try-start is the proof the lockout is on the right disconnect. A lock on the wrong breaker looks exactly like a lock on the right one until you press start. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Remove the head guard | Select | The guard comes off only now — locked, bled, try-started — and it comes off knowing it goes back on before anything else does. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Clear the jam | Carry and place | The jam is cleared by hand only because the belt cannot move. The carton goes to the reject stand, not back on the belt. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Refit the head guard | Select | The guard goes on before the lock comes off. In that order there is never a moment the nip is reachable and the belt can move. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Walk the line for defeated safeguards | Find (any order: pull-cord tied back) | A jam on a conveyor with a working pull-cord is a stop. On one with the cord tied back it is an injury. The walk is how the second kind gets found. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Reset the belt tension | Graded reading | The take-up was backed off to release energy; the belt runs at its tension or it slips and tracks off. Back into the band before restart. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Remove the lock, reset, restart | Select | Lock off last, by you. The line is cleared by eye before the start, because the start is the moment everything you did is tested. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `reach-nip` | You reached into the head pulley nip point with the belt live. A belt draws a hand into the pulley faster than you can pull back; the belt is locked and proven dead before anything goes near a nip. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `cord-tied` | You tied back the pull-cord so it stops tripping. The pull-cord is the only stop a worker on the line can reach; tied back, the next jam takes a hand with it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `guard-off-run` | You restarted with the head guard still off. A conveyor runs with every guard on, or it does not run — the guard is what makes the nip a place a hand cannot reach. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `ride-belt` | You stepped onto the belt to reach the jam. A belt is not a walkway; a restart, a slip, or the jam letting go puts you into the transfer chute. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Forklift Dock (SmartCiti.X · `forklift-dock`)

**Category:** Manufacturing & Automation  
**Trade:** Powered industrial truck operator — warehouse and dock  
**Certification / standard claimed:** OSHA 29 CFR 1910.178(l) powered industrial truck operator training and evaluation (three-year re-evaluation); ANSI/ITSDF B56.1 counterbalanced trucks; IBT (Teamsters) and UFCW warehouse locals' PIT programmes  
**Tagline:** Dock load-out: pre-shift inspection, belt on, trailer chocked and dock-locked before the plate, load against the capacity plate, forks under and mast back, low and steady, ramp in reverse, rack height, and the trailer that crept  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the pre-shift checklist | Select | The checklist is the record that the truck was inspected before the shift; the capacity plate is the number every load is measured against. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Pre-shift inspection | Sequence (any order: tires, forks and heel, mast chains, horn and lights) | A forklift with a cracked fork heel or a slack chain fails under the load it lifts, not in the yard. A truck that fails inspection is tagged out, not driven gently. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Belt on | Select | In a tip-over the overhead guard protects a belted operator. An unbelted one jumps, and the guard lands on them — the single biggest killer of forklift operators. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Secure the trailer | Sequence (any order: wheel chock, dock lock) | The chock stops creep; the lock stops a driver pulling away. Either one alone is the accident the other would have prevented. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Set the dock plate | Carry and place | The plate bridges the gap and the height difference. It sits on the trailer bed by at least four inches of lip, on a trailer that cannot move. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Check the trailer floor and nose | Select | A trailer floor rots from the inside. The truck weighs three times the load; a soft floor lets it through, and a dropped trailer with no landing-gear support noses down with the truck inside. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Check the load against the plate | Graded reading | The plate rates the truck at a 24-inch load centre. A long pallet moves the centre out and the rating down; the pallet that is 'about right' is the one that tips. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Pick the load | Sequence (strict order: forks fully under, mast tilted back, raised to travel height) | Under to the heel so the load cannot slide; back so it rests against the carriage; low so the centre of gravity stays inside the wheelbase. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Travel to the trailer | Tracked hold | A forklift stops in its own length only at walking pace. The horn at every door and aisle end is the pedestrian's only warning. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Take the ramp in reverse | Select | Loaded, the load faces uphill: forward up, reverse down. That keeps the weight over the drive axle and the load against the carriage instead of sliding off the tips. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Set the forks to the rack beam | Graded reading | Two inches over the beam clears it; a foot over is a load that drops onto the beam and a pallet that breaks. The height is set stopped, never while the truck is moving. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Walk the dock before the next load | Find (any order: trailer creep gap) | Every entry pushes the trailer. The dock is checked between loads, not at the end of the shift. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `ride-forks` | You let a coworker ride on the forks. A forklift has one seat and one belt; a person on the forks has neither, and the mast, the load and the overhead guard are all built on the assumption nobody is there. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `ramp-forward-loaded` | You drove forward down the ramp with a load. On a grade the load points downhill and the truck tips over the front axle; loaded, you go up forward and down in reverse. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `under-raised-load` | You walked under the raised load. Forks fail, chains stretch, pallets break — nothing that is lifted is ever stood under, and nothing raised is left unattended. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `no-chock-entry` | You drove into a trailer that was not chocked or dock-locked. A trailer creeps a few inches every time a truck enters; a driver hooks up and pulls away with you inside; the plate drops and the truck goes four feet to the yard. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## CNC Cell (SmartCiti.X · `cnc-cell`)

**Category:** Manufacturing & Automation  
**Trade:** CNC machinist — vertical machining centre  
**Certification / standard claimed:** IAM and USW machinist locals; NIMS Machining Level I CNC Milling (setup, operation, programming); OSHA 29 CFR 1910.212 machine guarding and 1910.147 lockout/tagout; ANSI B11.22 safety requirements for turning and milling centres; OSHA 1910.242(b) limiting compressed air for cleaning  
**Tagline:** Machining centre setup and first part: setup sheet, spindle stopped and locked before the envelope, workholding torqued, tool measured and offset, single-block dry run above the part, door closed, first article measured, chips brushed not blown  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the setup sheet | Select | The setup sheet is the contract between the programmer and the machinist. Everything measured, torqued or offset in this job is measured against a number on it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Make the machine safe to approach | Sequence (strict order: spindle stopped, mode to manual, lock and tag) | A stopped spindle is not a safe spindle while the control can still run a queued program. Manual mode and the lock are what make the envelope somewhere a hand can go. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Set the fixture and load the part | Sequence (strict order: fixture seated on the table, fixture bolted and located, part loaded in the vise) | Everything downstream assumes the fixture has not moved. A chip under a fixture is a tenth of an inch of error on every part in the run. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Torque the workholding | Graded reading | Under-clamped, the part comes out of the vise at cutting speed. Over-clamped, thin-wall stock distorts and every dimension is wrong once the clamp is released. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Inspect and load the tool | Sequence (any order: tool and holder inspected, tool loaded in the spindle) | A cracked insert or a loose pull stud at spindle speed becomes shrapnel inside a sheet-metal enclosure that was never designed to stop it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Measure the tool and set the offset | Graded reading | The length offset is what tells the control where the tip of this tool is. Wrong by a tenth and the first move either cuts air or drives the tool through the fixture. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Single-block dry run above the part | Tracked hold | The dry run is where a wrong offset, a missed clamp or a rapid into the fixture shows up harmlessly. Every setup gets one, every time, and it is held above the part so a mistake costs nothing. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Close the door | Select | The enclosure is the guard. Closed and interlocked, it contains a part that comes loose; open, it is a window a part leaves through at the speed it was spinning. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Run the first part | Turn | The operator stays at the panel with a hand near the feed hold for the whole first part, because the first part is the one that finds the mistake the dry run missed. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Measure the first article | Graded reading | The first article is what says the setup makes good parts. Nothing else in the run is measured until this one is signed off, because everything after it is a copy of it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Clear the chips properly | Carry and place | A brush and a vacuum, never air and never a hand. That single habit is most of what separates a machinist with ten fingers from one without. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Walk the machine before the run | Find (any order: coolant below the sight glass, torn way cover) | A production run is hours of unattended cutting. What is marginal at the first part is a failure by the hundredth. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `hand-in-envelope` | You reached into the work envelope with the spindle live. A machining centre restarts on a queued command and a tool at four thousand revolutions does not care what is in the way; the door interlock exists because hands are faster than judgement and slower than a spindle. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `air-hose-chips` | You blew the chips off with the air hose. Compressed air drives a chip through skin and into an eye, throws coolant mist into the air you are breathing, and packs swarf into the ways where it scores them. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `bare-hand-chips` | You pulled the chip nest off with a bare hand. Machining chips are a continuous razor at the temperature they came off the cut, and a stringer wrapped on a finger takes the finger with it if the spindle moves. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `cycle-door-open` | You pressed cycle start with the door open. The interlock is the only thing between the operator and a part that comes out of the vise at cutting speed; defeating it is the classic machine-shop fatality. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

# Building Systems & Facilities

## Chiller Plant (SmartCiti.X · `chiller-plant`)

**Category:** Building Systems & Facilities  
**Trade:** HVAC / refrigeration technician  
**Certification / standard claimed:** UA — EPA Section 608 Universal refrigerant certified  
**Tagline:** District chiller isolation, refrigerant recovery and confined mechanical space entry  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the work order | Select | Different refrigerants have different recovery procedures and pressure ranges. Working from memory on the wrong type is how recovery cylinders get over-pressurised. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Check the plant room atmosphere | Graded reading | This is checked before you do anything else in the room, every single time you enter — not just when something smells wrong, because a refrigerant leak does not smell like anything. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Shut down the chiller from the HMI | Select | A controlled shutdown lets refrigerant equalise and the compressor spin down safely, instead of isolating a running machine under load. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Isolate electrical power | Turn | A stopped compressor can restart on a call for cooling if the electrics are still live. Isolation is what makes 'stopped' permanent for the duration of your work. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Lock the disconnect | Select | Your lock, your control. A chiller that gets re-energised by a building management system override is still your problem if your hands are inside it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Close the service valves | Sequence (strict order: liquid line service valve, suction line service valve) | Closing liquid first traps the charge on the high side where the recovery machine can pull it efficiently, rather than pushing it all to one side at once. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Connect the gauge manifold | Select | The manifold is how you actually see high-side and low-side pressure through the recovery, instead of guessing when it is done. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Recover the refrigerant | Graded reading | Recovery to the certified vacuum level is what the regulations require and what actually gets the charge safely into the cylinder instead of into the room. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Verify the recovery cylinder | Select | A recovery cylinder filled past its rated capacity has no vapour space left to absorb pressure change — it becomes a hydraulic hazard as ambient temperature rises. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Complete the compressor repair | Select | With the circuit fully recovered and pressure at vacuum, the compressor can finally be opened without refrigerant or oil discharge. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Evacuate, recharge and restart | Select | A system with damp air pulled in during the repair will not perform and will corrode from the inside — the vacuum step is not optional even when you are in a hurry. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `vent-to-air` | You are venting refrigerant straight to atmosphere. It is both a regulatory violation and a physical hazard — refrigerant is heavier than air and pools in the low points of exactly the plant room you are standing in. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `low-oxygen-room` | You walked past the oxygen monitor already in alarm. A large refrigerant leak displaces breathable air with no smell and no colour — the monitor is the only warning you get. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `pressurized-fitting` | You are about to crack that fitting under full system pressure. High-side refrigerant pressure through a sudden gap is a liquid and gas jet that causes frostbite burns and can eject the fitting itself. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `hot-compressor` | That compressor housing is at operating temperature and you have no gloves on. Contact burns from compressor casings are common and completely preventable. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Boiler Room (SmartCiti.X · `boiler-room`)

**Category:** Building Systems & Facilities  
**Trade:** Stationary engineer / steamfitter  
**Certification / standard claimed:** IUOE — state-licensed Stationary Engineer, boiler operation  
**Tagline:** Boiler lockout, confined-space firebox entry and a controlled re-light  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the work order | Select | The work order names exactly which fuel and steam lines this repair needs isolated. Working from memory on a multi-boiler plant is how the wrong line gets left live. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Shut the boiler down | Select | A controlled shutdown lets the burner cycle down cleanly instead of isolating fuel and steam lines on a boiler that is still firing. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Confirm the drum has cooled and depressurised | Graded reading | A boiler drum holds stored heat and pressure long after the burner stops. The safe band is what actually makes the inspection port and the firebox approachable, not the elapsed time since shutdown. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Isolate the fuel train — double block and bleed | Sequence (strict order: upstream fuel block valve, downstream fuel block valve, bleed valve) | Two closed valves with a vented bleed point between them is what proves the fuel train is actually isolated — the bleed valve venting nothing is your confirmation, not a guess based on two closed handles. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Isolate the main steam stop valve | Turn | The steam side gets isolated independently of the fuel side — a firebox repair with the steam header still connected leaves a pressurised path back into the drum you are working next to. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Lock out the isolation points | Select | The locks are what stop the fuel or steam isolation from being reversed by someone else on shift while you are standing in the firebox. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Test the firebox atmosphere | Graded reading | A firebox that has just been fired holds residual combustion gases that do not clear on their own timeline you can guess at — the meter is what actually proves it is breathable. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Open the confined-space entry permit | Select | The firebox is a confined space in its own right, separate from the boiler lockout. It gets its own permit and its own attendant before anyone's shoulders go through that port. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Open the inspection port | Select | This is the point where every control before it pays off — cooled, depressurised, isolated, tested. Open it any earlier and at least one of those is still unresolved. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Complete the refractory repair | Select | The repair only happens once the space is confirmed cold, isolated and breathable — that is the entire reason the steps ahead of this one exist. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Remove the isolation locks | Select | Locks come off only once the firebox is confirmed empty and the port is closed — removing isolation while anyone could still be inside defeats the reason the locks went on. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Run the controlled re-light | Select | A purge cycle clears any fuel vapour that collected during the work before the igniter ever fires — skipping straight to ignition is how a re-light becomes a firebox explosion. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `hot-inspection-port` | You are opening the inspection port before pressure and temperature are confirmed safe. Flash steam and superheated water erupting from a pressurised drum cause burns before you can pull your hand back. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `live-fuel-valve` | That fuel valve is not actually isolated. Working on the burner train with a live gas or oil supply behind it means one dropped spark or one hot surface away from an explosion. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `firebox-no-test` | You are entering the firebox before the atmosphere has been tested. Residual combustion gases in an unventilated firebox displace oxygen with no smell to warn you before you are already affected. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `relight-not-clear` | You are relighting the burner without confirming the isolation locks are removed and everyone is clear. A relight with a lock still in place or a person still inside the firebox can ignite trapped fuel or catch someone in the burner path. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Elevator Pit (SmartCiti.X · `elevator-pit`)

**Category:** Building Systems & Facilities  
**Trade:** Elevator constructor / mechanic  
**Certification / standard claimed:** IUEC — NAESA QEI-qualified elevator mechanic  
**Tagline:** Pit and car-top entry: main line lockout, dual stop switches and governor inspection  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Open the work permit and post out-of-service | Select | The signage is what keeps a passenger from calling a car that a mechanic is standing inside. It goes up before the disconnect comes down. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Open the main line disconnect | Turn | The disconnect is the isolation. Nothing in the pit or on the car top is approached before the drive has no source of power. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Lock and tag the disconnect | Select | Your lock says the isolation belongs to you specifically. Nobody re-energises this car while your lock is still on the switch. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Verify zero energy at the controller | Graded reading | The disconnect being open is not proof by itself. You confirm zero energy the same way for every isolation, every time, before anyone goes near the shaft. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Engage the pit stop switch | Select | The machine room disconnect and the pit switch are two independent controls. The pit switch is the one you can see holding from inside the pit itself. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Descend into the pit | Select | Entry happens after the switch is confirmed engaged, never on the assumption that the machine room disconnect alone is enough. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Inspect the buffers and pit ladder | Find (any order: cracked buffer, corroded ladder rung, flooded sump pit) | A pit inspection is a search for what has quietly failed since the last visit, not a checklist tick from the doorway. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Engage the car-top stop switch | Select | This is the second independent switch. The pit switch protects the pit; this one protects whoever is standing on the car itself. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Open the car-top access and enter inspection mode | Select | Inspection mode caps the car's speed and puts operation under your hand at the car-top station, not under a passenger call. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Check the governor rope tension | Graded reading | A governor that cannot pull free at the right tension will not trip the safeties at overspeed — this is checked on its own, not assumed from the rope looking fine. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Restore in the correct order | Sequence (strict order: car-top stop switch, pit stop switch, main line disconnect) | Release from the top down: you confirm the car top clear before the pit, and the pit clear before power goes back to the drive. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Run a test operation before release | Select | The car is not returned to service on the assumption the work went well — it is proven with an actual run first. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `counterweight-zone` | You stepped into the counterweight runby without the pit stop-switch confirmed engaged. If the switch is not truly holding the drive, an arriving car or counterweight gives no warning and no time to get clear — crush and shear injuries here are almost always fatal. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `cartop-edge` | You leaned past the car-top rail with the car-top stop switch not yet engaged. The pit switch does not protect someone standing on the car itself — an unswitched car can still answer a call and carry you into the overhead clearance. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `frayed-governor-rope` | That governor rope has visibly broken wires that were ignored during inspection. A frayed rope can slip or part before it ever pulls the safeties, which is exactly the failure the governor exists to catch. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `quick-restore-switch` | That is a shortcut restore switch, not the proper sequence. Closing it re-energises the car before anyone has confirmed the pit and car top are clear of people and the locks have come off in order. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Fire Pump (SmartCiti.X · `fire-pump`)

**Category:** Building Systems & Facilities  
**Trade:** Fire sprinkler fitter / fire pump technician  
**Certification / standard claimed:** UA — journeyman sprinkler fitter; NFPA 25 (inspection, testing and maintenance of water-based systems) annual fire pump flow test; NICET Inspection & Testing of Water-Based Systems  
**Tagline:** Annual pump flow test: alarm notification, controller to manual, test header to safe discharge, churn / 100% / 150% points, curve compared, system restored  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the test record and the nameplate curve | Select | The nameplate says what the pump is supposed to do; last year's record says what it did. This year's test is judged against both. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Notify the alarm company and the building | Select | The pump running sends signals. The monitoring company holds the dispatch for the test window, and takes the account off test when you call back. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Confirm suction and the supply valve open | Graded reading | The pump needs water at its suction before it is called. A closed valve or a dropped supply shows here first, not after the impeller is gone. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Lay the test hoses to a safe discharge | Sequence (strict order: hose lines from the test header, discharge to the outside, playpipe nozzles with pitot access) | The test flows the pump's full capacity. Where that water goes is decided before the header opens — outside, secured, away from people and drains that cannot take it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Controller to manual test | Turn | In manual, the pump runs on your command and stops on it. In automatic, a pressure drop starts it before you are ready and a timer stops it mid-reading. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Start the pump at churn | Select | Churn — no flow — is the first point on the curve. It also proves the pump starts, comes to speed and holds pressure before any water moves. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Read the churn pressure | Graded reading | Churn pressure above nameplate means the relief valve is doing its job; well below means a worn impeller or the wrong speed. It is the first number on the sheet. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Open the header to 100% flow | Graded reading | Rated flow at rated pressure is the pump's nameplate point. This is the reading that says the pump is still the pump it was sold as. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Hold the 150% flow point | Timed hold | At 150% the pump must still make 65% of rated pressure. The readings are taken with the flow held steady, not on the way past it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Read current and speed | Sequence (any order: motor current, pump speed) | Pressure alone can hide a slipping motor or a pump running slow. Amps and speed are what tie the pressure to the curve. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Close the header, controller to automatic, call the alarm company back | Select | A fire pump left in manual will not start for a fire. The restoration is the last step of the test and the most important one. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Walk the pump room before locking up | Find (any order: packing gland running dry) | The test heats everything up and shows what the room hides. The walk after the run is where a small fault gets caught before it is a failed pump. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `no-notify-run` | You started the pump without notifying the alarm company. A fire pump running trips the waterflow and pump-running signals — the fire department rolls to a building with no fire, and the next real signal from this building gets a slower response. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `flow-indoors` | You opened the test header with the discharge pointed into the pump room. Two thousand gallons a minute at 100 psi fills the room, floods the controller and knocks a person off their feet; the hose lines go to the outside discharge. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `suction-closed` | You started the pump with the suction control valve closed. A centrifugal pump with no water at the suction cavitates and destroys its impeller and seal in minutes — the suction gauge is read before the pump is called. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `bypass-controller` | You jumpered the controller to force the pump on. The controller is the listed device that starts, runs and protects the pump; jumpered, it has no overload, no phase protection and no record of the run. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Cooling Tower (SmartCiti.X · `cooling-tower`)

**Category:** Building Systems & Facilities  
**Trade:** Stationary engineer — water treatment and cooling towers  
**Certification / standard claimed:** IUOE Local stationary engineer; ASHRAE 188 building water management program and Guideline 12 Legionella control; CDC / OSHA Legionella toolkit for cooling towers; OSHA 29 CFR 1910.134 respiratory protection and 1910.147 lockout/tagout; EPA FIFRA-registered biocide label compliance  
**Tagline:** Quarterly clean and Legionella control: plan and last culture read, tower down and fan locked, respirator before the basin, biofilm physically removed, drift eliminator checked, biocide shock to the label, conductivity and halogen back in range, log signed  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the water management plan | Select | ASHRAE 188 makes the plan the document the building is judged against. Every limit, every dose and every log entry in this task comes from it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Read the last culture | Select | A rising count changes the task from routine to remedial. The number decides whether this is a clean or a disinfection, and whether the building's risk group has to be told. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Shut down and lock out the fan | Sequence (strict order: tower off at the panel, fan breaker open, lock and tag) | A fan that restarts on a building-automation call while someone is in the basin is both a mechanical hazard and an aerosol release. The lock is what makes the shutdown real. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Put on respiratory and chemical protection | Sequence (any order: half-face respirator with P100, coveralls and chemical gloves, eyewash station checked) | Two exposures on this job: the aerosol from the basin and the concentrated biocide. The respirator handles one and the gloves and eyewash handle the other. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Drain the basin | Turn | The basin is drained to sanitary, never to the storm drain, because it is treated water with a biocide residual and whatever has been growing in it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Wet-wash the basin and fill | Timed hold | The clean is physical: the biofilm has to come off the surface, wet, at low pressure so nothing goes airborne. A biocide over a biofilm does not reach the organism living under it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Inspect the drift eliminator and sump | Find (any order: gap in the drift eliminator, debris in the sump screen) | The drift eliminator is the single most important piece of Legionella control hardware in the tower: it is what stops the aerosol leaving. A gap in it undoes every chemical in the plan. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Dose the biocide shock | Graded reading | The label rate is calculated from the basin volume, and it is legally binding. Under it, the shock is theatre; over it, the discharge and the tower metal both pay. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Hold the contact time | Tracked hold | Kill is concentration multiplied by time. Dropping the concentration halfway through is the same as not dosing, and the organism comes back in the biofilm that survived. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Test free halogen | Graded reading | The tower goes back in service at the operating residual, not the shock residual. That is the number the plan's control limit is written against and the number the log records. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Return to service and sign the log | Sequence (strict order: lock removed, tower back in service, log signed and next culture booked) | The log is the building's defence and its memory. An unsigned service is an unperformed service the first time a health department asks. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `fan-live-basin` | You opened the basin with the fan running. A disturbed basin aerosolises everything in it, and the fan puts that plume over the roof, the intake and the street — this is exactly how a building outbreak starts. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `dry-brush-biofilm` | You dry-brushed the biofilm. Dry scale and biofilm go airborne as respirable dust carrying the organism; the surfaces are kept wet through the whole clean for that reason. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `no-respirator` | You went into the basin with no respiratory protection. A cooling tower basin at cleaning is the highest-exposure task in the building, and a surgical mask is not respiratory protection. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `dose-off-label` | You dosed the biocide off the label rate. The label is enforceable law under FIFRA: under-dose and the shock does nothing to the biofilm, over-dose and the discharge is a permit violation and a corrosion event. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

# Construction & Structural Trades

## Steel Erector (SmartCiti.X · `steel-erector`)

**Category:** Construction & Structural Trades  
**Trade:** Ironworker / structural steel connector  
**Certification / standard claimed:** Ironworkers — OSHA 29 CFR 1926 Subpart R qualified connector  
**Tagline:** Structural steel connecting: fall protection, tag-line control and the bolt-up sequence  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the erection plan | Select | The raising gang works one pick at a time from a sequence the engineer set for a reason — steel goes up out of order and the structure is not stable for the next pick. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Connect to the horizontal lifeline | Select | The lifeline runs the length of the beam you are about to walk. Nothing else on a structural floor is rated to catch you before it exists. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Inspect the harness and positioning lanyard | Select | A connector's harness gets checked at the start of every shift it is worn on steel — this is not equipment you find out has failed after it already has. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Confirm crane signal protocol | Select | The operator cannot see the connection point from the cab. Every signal that moves the load has to mean the same thing to everyone on the pick or the load moves on a guess. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Scan the deck for hazards | Find (any order: unmarked deck opening, cracked temporary plank, loose decking sheet) | A deck hazard scan happens before you commit to walking a section, because the deck that looked finished from the ladder is not the same thing as a deck that has actually been checked. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Cover and barricade the opening | Carry and place | A found hazard that is not corrected is just a hazard you now feel less responsible for. The cover goes on before anyone else reaches that section of deck. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Guide the suspended column with the tag line | Timed hold | The tag line keeps your hands a rope's length from a ton of moving steel while still giving you enough control to place it precisely. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Land the member on the splice plate | Select | The splice plate is machined to take the column's base exactly once it is aligned — forcing a mismatched landing bends the plate before the first bolt goes in. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Plumb and align the column | Graded reading | Every connection above this one inherits whatever lean you accept here. Plumb now is the only point in the sequence where it is still cheap to fix. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Install bolts in the required order | Sequence (strict order: temporary bolt A, temporary bolt B, erection bolt) | Temporary bolts hold the connection snug while it is checked and plumbed. The erection bolt goes in last, once the joint is already stable enough that adding it does not shift the alignment. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Apply final torque | Select | Final torque only happens once the minimum number of bolts is in and the connection is confirmed plumb — it is the last thing that happens, never a shortcut to skip the temporary bolts. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Log the connection complete | Select | The crane hook does not come off until the connection is logged as self-supporting. The record is what the inspector and the next raising gang inherit. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `suspended-load-path` | You are standing under the load path. A suspended steel member has nothing holding it up but a sling and a hook — if either lets go, you are standing exactly where it lands. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `open-floor-hole` | That deck opening has no cover and no barricade. On a structural floor, a leading edge you cannot see coming is exactly how a fall happens on a surface that looks solid from three steps away. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `hand-guided-member` | You grabbed the swinging member with your hand instead of the tag line. A ton of suspended steel does not stop when your grip does — it keeps moving and takes your hand, or you, with it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `unclipped-leading-edge` | You stepped to the leading edge without your lanyard connected to the overhead lifeline. At height, the edge of unfinished decking is a fall with nothing between you and the ground below. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Crane Yard (SmartCiti.X · `crane-yard`)

**Category:** Construction & Structural Trades  
**Trade:** Mobile crane operator / rigger  
**Certification / standard claimed:** IUOE — NCCCO Mobile Crane Operator certified  
**Tagline:** Mobile crane pick: outrigger setup, load chart verification, anti-collision zone programming and a tag-line controlled lift  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the lift plan | Select | Every control that follows is sized to this specific pick. A lift plan read after the outriggers are already down is a plan you are checking your own work against. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Extend and set the outriggers | Sequence (any order: front outriggers, rear outriggers) | The outriggers are what turns the crane's rated capacity from a number on paper into something the ground under this specific pad can actually support. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Check the crane is level | Graded reading | A crane that is off level derates in the direction of the lean without saying so. The chart assumes level ground — this is where you prove it, not assume it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Verify the load chart for this pick | Graded reading | The chart is read at the actual radius and boom angle for this specific pick, not the radius from last week's job that felt about the same. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Teach the anti-collision boundary | Sequence (strict order: boundary corner A, boundary corner B, boundary corner C) | The RCI's anti-collision system only knows about a boundary you actually taught it. Teach the corners out of order and the system draws the line through the wrong side of the property, not around it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Save the boundary to the RCI | Select | A taught boundary sitting unsaved is just where the boom happened to point. Saving it is what makes the RCI actually cut the swing at that line instead of trusting the operator to remember it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Test-swing and verify the cutout | Timed hold | You verify a brand-new anti-collision zone at slow test speed before the actual pick, because the first time it should ever stop the boom for real is the first time it's asked to. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Select the correctly rated sling | Select | A sling's tag capacity assumes it is undamaged. The rack always has more than one option — the rated, sound one is the only correct choice. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Inspect the shackle | Select | A shackle with a worn or bent pin can walk loose under a cyclical load. The stamp tells you what it is rated for; the inspection tells you whether it still is. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Verify the sling leg angle | Select | A shallow sling angle multiplies the tension each leg actually carries. The same sling that is comfortably rated at a steep angle can be overloaded at a shallow one carrying the identical weight. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Confirm the signal person protocol | Select | Only one person directs the lift. The operator moves on that person's signal alone, so everyone on the pick needs to know who that is before the load leaves the ground. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Hook the rigging to the load | Select | A load that is not centred under the hook swings the instant it clears the ground, which is exactly the moment you have the least control over it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 13 | Guide the lift with the tag line | Timed hold | The tag line lets you steer a suspended load without ever putting a hand on it. A load with no one steering it swings on its own until something stops it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 14 | Make a controlled set-down | Carry and place | A controlled set-down means the load is stable on its own before the rigging comes off — not lowered fast and released before anyone has checked it is actually sitting flat. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 15 | Close out the lift log | Select | The lift is not finished when the load lands — it is finished when the rigging is recovered, the area is confirmed clear and the pick is logged for the next crew. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `swing-radius-stand` | You are standing inside the crane's swing radius while it is rigged for a pick. The counterweight and boom sweep that space without warning a person on foot can react to in time. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `two-block-risk` | You are hoisting past the anti-two-block limit. Running the hook block into the boom tip parts the hoist line or snaps the boom itself, and the load falls the moment either one lets go. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `chart-exceeded` | That pick is logged past the chart's rated capacity for this radius and boom angle. Cranes tip or fail structurally at the edge of the chart, not with a comfortable margin of warning first. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `kinked-sling` | That sling has a visible kink in its body. A kink is a stress point the sling was never rated for — it can fail well under its tag capacity exactly where the kink is. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
