## Trench Box (SmartCiti.X · `trench-box`)

**Category:** Construction & Structural Trades  
**Trade:** Laborer / excavation and shoring  
**Certification / standard claimed:** LIUNA — OSHA 29 CFR 1926 Subpart P Competent Person  
**Tagline:** Excavation shoring: competent-person inspection, atmosphere testing and protective systems  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Guard the excavation | Sequence (any order: cone at the approach, cone at the far end, trench guard rail) | An open trench next to foot traffic or vehicle movement is a hazard to everyone who is not on this crew, guarded before the first assessment even starts. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Read the excavation permit | Select | The permit sets the protective system for this trench from its soil classification and depth, not from what looked adequate on the last job. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Complete the competent-person inspection | Select | A competent person checks the actual walls in front of them every day conditions could have changed — after rain, after a freeze-thaw, after any nearby vibration. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Set the spoil pile back from the edge | Select | Spoil piled at the edge adds surcharge load exactly where the wall is already carrying the most stress. Setting it back removes that extra weight from the equation entirely. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Test the trench atmosphere | Graded reading | Tested before every entry, not just the first one of the day — a nicked gas line or a change in groundwater can turn a trench's atmosphere hours after it was last checked. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Place the trench box | Carry and place | The box is rated for this depth and soil class from the permit. It goes in before entry, not as a precaution added after someone is already working below grade. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Place the access ladder | Select | A ladder within 25 feet means an exit is never more than a few steps away, in the trench or on the surface, in an emergency that gives you no time to walk further. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Maintain contact with the spotter | Timed hold | The spotter watches the walls, the spoil pile and the surrounding area the whole time you cannot, because your attention below grade is on the pipe, not the edge. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Complete the utility line repair | Select | The actual task only starts once guarding, inspection, atmosphere and shoring are all already in place — not run in parallel with them to save time. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Teach the GPS grade-control waypoints | Sequence (strict order: Start waypoint, Mid waypoint, End waypoint) | The automated grader follows these points in the order they're recorded, not the order that seems obvious — teach them start to end, matching the pipe's actual slope. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Save the grade-control profile | Select | An untaught point list is just recorded positions. Saving it is what turns three grade points into a profile the grader can actually run against. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Dry-run the grade-control profile | Timed hold | A brand-new profile is verified at a walk-through pace with a hand on the console, watching the whole run, before the grader ever moves unattended over a trench a crew just worked in. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 13 | Take a headcount before backfill | Select | A headcount against the crew list is what confirms the trench is actually empty — not an assumption because the ladder looked clear from the surface. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 14 | Remove the protective box | Select | The box comes out only after everyone is confirmed clear of the trench — removing protection with anyone still below grade defeats the entire point of having placed it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 15 | Backfill and compact | Select | Backfilling in compacted lifts is what keeps the surface from settling later and undoing the repair that was just made underneath it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `unshored-entry` | You stepped into the trench before the protective system was placed. A cubic metre of soil weighs more than a small car, and an unprotected wall can let go with no warning at all before it happens. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `spoil-too-close` | That spoil pile is sitting right on the edge. The extra surcharge load from a pile that close is exactly what pushes a marginal trench wall past the point it can hold itself up. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `no-ladder-access` | There is no ladder within reach of this section. If the wall lets go or the atmosphere turns, everyone below grade needs an exit inside seconds, not a walk to find one. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `untested-entry` | You are entering before the atmosphere has been tested. A trench can fill with oxygen-deficient or toxic air from a nicked utility line with no smell and no colour to warn you first. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Scaffold Erection (SmartCiti.X · `scaffold-erection`)

**Category:** Construction & Structural Trades  
**Trade:** Scaffold erector / carpenter  
**Certification / standard claimed:** UBC (Carpenters) — scaffold erector qualification; OSHA 29 CFR 1926.451 / 1926.454 scaffold competent person; fall protection during erection per 1926.451(g)  
**Tagline:** Frame scaffold, two lifts: ground and sills, base plates, plumb and level, tie-ins, full planking, guardrails, ladder access, green tag  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the scaffold plan | Select | The plan is the competent person's design: how high, how tied, how loaded. The erection follows it; it does not improvise on site. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Inspect the ground | Select | Everything above the sills stands on this. Soft ground under one leg settles under load, and the scaffold racks toward it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Set the mudsills and base plates | Sequence (strict order: left mudsill, right mudsill, base plates on the sills) | The sill spreads the leg load over the ground; the base plate sits on the sill, never on the ground or a block. In that order, so the jacks have something to stand on. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Stand the first lift | Carry and place | The first lift sets everything above it. It goes on the plates square, braced both sides, before anyone lets go of it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Plumb and level the first lift | Graded reading | Out of plumb at the first lift is doubled at the second. The jacks are adjusted now, while the scaffold is one lift high and light. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Tie the scaffold to the structure | Timed hold | The tie is what makes the scaffold part of the building instead of a tower beside it. It goes in at the first lift, before the second goes up. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Stand the second lift | Carry and place | The second lift goes on a plumb, level, tied first lift. Pins in so the frames cannot lift off; braces on so they cannot rack. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Fully plank the working platform | Sequence (strict order: outside plank, middle plank, wall-side plank) | Full planking is the floor the crew works on. A gap is a leg through the platform; an unsecured plank is a plank that walks off the ledger. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Guardrails and toeboards | Sequence (any order: top rail, mid rail, toeboard) | Ten feet up, the guardrail is the fall protection. Top rail stops a person, mid rail stops a slip under it, toeboard stops the hammer going over onto whoever is below. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Fit the access ladder | Select | The ladder is how the scaffold is used, not just built. Without it, the first person up climbs the braces — and so does everyone after. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Competent person inspection | Find (any order: missing coupling pin) | The tag is the competent person's word that the scaffold is safe. The inspection is the work behind the word. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Hang the green tag | Select | Green says built and inspected; anyone may use it. Yellow says restrictions; red says stay off. The colour at the ladder is the whole crew's instruction. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `climb-frames` | You climbed the cross-braces to reach the second lift. Braces are not a ladder; they flex, they are not spaced for feet, and the fall is onto the frames below. Access is a ladder or a stair, built in. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `untied-work` | You started planking the second lift with no tie to the structure. Two lifts of frame scaffold with no tie is a free-standing tower over its height-to-base ratio — the wind, or one person leaning, is enough. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `block-on-sill` | You set a base plate on a concrete block instead of a mudsill. Blocks crack and tip; a base plate on a tipped block puts a leg in the air and the load on the other three. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `plank-gap` | You left a plank gap over the wall side wide enough to step through. Full planking means no gap over 1 inch at the wall, and none anywhere a foot can go. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Concrete Pour (SmartCiti.X · `concrete-pour`)

**Category:** Construction & Structural Trades  
**Trade:** Cement mason / concrete finisher and laborer  
**Certification / standard claimed:** OPCMIA cement masons and LIUNA laborers; ACI Concrete Field Testing Technician Grade I (slump ASTM C143, air C231, cylinders C31); OSHA 29 CFR 1926 Subpart Q concrete and masonry (1926.701 impalement protection, 1926.703 formwork); ACI 347 formwork pressure and OSHA 1926.1153 respirable crystalline silica  
**Tagline:** Wall pour from a boom pump: pre-pour walk, rebar capped and forms braced, slump and air tested before the first yard, nobody under the boom, placement rate inside the form pressure, vibrated not segregated, cylinders cast, finish on the clock  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the pour card | Select | Formwork is engineered for a rate of rise, not a volume. The card carries that number, the mix, and the temperature window the pour is allowed in. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Walk the pour before the truck arrives | Find (any order: uncapped vertical rebar, unpinned form brace, debris in the form) | Everything found now is a five-minute fix. The same things found once concrete is moving are a stopped pour, a cold joint, or somebody in the ambulance. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Correct what the walk found | Sequence (any order: rebar caps fitted, brace pinned, form blown out) | A defect found and not fixed is worse than one never found, because now the crew believes the walk was done. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Slump test the first truck | Graded reading | Slump is the check that the mix on site is the mix that was ordered. Too stiff will not fill the form; too wet has had water added on site and has lost the strength the engineer specified. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Air content and cylinders | Sequence (strict order: air content measured, test cylinders cast) | Entrained air is what survives a freeze-thaw winter, and the cylinders are the only proof of strength anyone will have in twenty-eight days. Both are taken from the same load, before placement. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Clear the boom zone | Select | The zone under a charged line is the one place on a pour nobody has a reason to be. A plug clears as a whip, and it does it when the pressure finally breaks it, not when you are watching. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Place the first lift | Tracked hold | The rate of rise is what sets the pressure on the form. Placing fast in one corner is how a wall form blows, and it is the most common formwork failure there is. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Consolidate the lift | Timed hold | Vibration drives out entrapped air; over-vibration drives the aggregate down and the paste up, which is segregation. Straight in, straight out, and never used to move concrete sideways. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Set the construction joint key | Turn | The key is what makes the next lift act as one wall with this one. It goes in while the concrete is still plastic, and the surface is roughened and cleaned before the next pour. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Wait out the bleed water | Graded reading | Finishing while bleed water is on the surface works that water back into the top, and the top is the part that has to last. This is the single most common way a slab is ruined. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Float, edge and cure | Sequence (strict order: bull float, edger and jointer, curing applied) | Curing is not cleanup, it is the last structural step: concrete that dries instead of curing loses a large part of its strength and crazes on the surface. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Cut the hardened spill wet | Carry and place | Cutting concrete dry is a silica exposure many times over the limit. Water on the blade or a vacuum shroud is not optional, and it is the difference between a nuisance and silicosis. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `under-the-boom` | You stood under the pump boom with the line charged. A plugged line clears as a whip: the hose end swings hard enough to kill, and the plug lets go without warning when the pressure finally breaks it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `uncapped-rebar` | You worked over uncapped vertical rebar. A fall of any height onto an uncapped bar is an impalement; the caps are rated for it and they cost less than the ambulance. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `overfill-forms` | You kept placing after the forms started to bow. Formwork fails all at once, not gradually, and it buries whoever is inside the pour line under tonnes of wet concrete. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `dry-cut-silica` | You dry-cut the hardened spill with no water and no vacuum. Cutting concrete dry puts respirable crystalline silica in the air at many times the permissible limit, and silicosis does not get better. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

# Entertainment & Live Events

## Rigging Loft (SmartCiti.X · `rigging-loft`)

**Category:** Entertainment & Live Events  
**Trade:** Theatrical rigger (IATSE)  
**Certification / standard claimed:** IATSE — ETCP Certified Rigger, Arena  
**Tagline:** Counterweight fly-system operation: arbor inspection, balance, cued flying and automation cue programming  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the rigging plot | Select | The plot is the plan for this line set specifically — capacity, load and trim height are not the same from one batten to the next. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Walk the counterweight system | Find (any order: unlocked arbor on line 3, frayed lift line on line 5, unsecured counterweight brick) | A pre-show rigging check is a search across every line set on the rail, not a glance at the one you're about to fly. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Check comms with the fly floor | Select | Every cue on this rail runs over that channel. If it isn't clear before you start, nobody downstairs hears 'clear' or 'moving' when it matters. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Post a floor watch | Select | The floor watch is a second set of eyes on the one place a rigger at the rail cannot see — directly beneath the load. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Lower the batten to load position | Select | Counterweight goes on at the deck, not at height. The batten comes down to a working position before anything is loaded onto it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Check the batten's rated capacity | Select | The plate is the hard limit for this specific batten and its rope set. The plot's planned load is checked against it before a single brick goes in. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Load the arbor to balance | Graded reading | A balanced arbor is what lets one person hold a loaded line at the rail. Under- or over-loaded, the rope does the deciding instead of the operator. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Lock the arbor rail | Select | The lock is what keeps a balanced arbor from drifting the moment you step away from the rail. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Call the fly cue | Sequence (strict order: call clear, call moving, call stopped) | Clear has to come back before moving is ever called — moving is a commitment that the deck is not still checking under the line. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Lock off at trim height | Select | The trim pin is the second, physical lock that holds the line set exactly where the cue left it, independent of the rail lock. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Teach the automation winch's cue positions | Sequence (strict order: park position, mid-show position, full-out position) | A motorized automation line plays back a taught cue exactly as recorded — teach full-out before mid-show and the batten runs straight past the position an actor is expecting it to stop at, on stage, live. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Save the automation cue | Select | An untaught position list is just where the winch happened to stop. Saving it as a cue is what lets the board operator fire it with one button during the actual show. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 13 | Run the cue at rehearsal speed and verify the deck | Timed hold | You run a brand-new automation cue at rehearsal speed with your hand on the console before it ever fires at full speed during a live show with the cast on stage. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 14 | Sign the rigging log | Select | The log is what the next call tells the next operator — load, trim and lock status, not a memory of how the show went. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `standing-under-load` | You are standing directly beneath a loaded batten and arbor. A parted rope or a dropped counterweight brick falls fast and gives no warning — nobody works or walks under a flown line while it can still move. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `unlocked-rail` | That arbor's locking rail pin is out and the line set is not secured. An unlocked arbor can run away the moment the rope weight shifts, and it takes whatever is in its path with it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `shortcut-call` | That is an override call button that skips the clearance check. Calling 'moving' before the deck confirms clear is how a batten comes down on someone who never heard the cue. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `excess-bricks` | That stack adds weight past the batten's rated capacity. An overloaded line set can overhaul the operator at the rail, part a rope, or bring the batten down out of control. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Stage Power (SmartCiti.X · `stage-power`)

**Category:** Entertainment & Live Events  
**Trade:** Stage / touring electrician  
**Certification / standard claimed:** IATSE — ETCP Certified Entertainment Electrician; NEC Article 520 (theaters) and 525 / single-pole separable connector (cam-lock) sequence; NFPA 70E qualified for the verification  
**Tagline:** Company switch tie-in: lockout, live-dead-live, cam-locks ground-first, cover and strain relief, energise, phase check, load test  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the power plot | Select | The plot says what the venue can give and what the rig needs. A 400 A switch feeding a 600 A rig is a decision made here, not when the breaker trips at the top of the show. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Open and lock the company switch | Turn | The switch is opened and locked before the door comes off. Your lock is the only thing that stops the house electrician energising it while your hands are on the lugs. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Arc-rated PPE for the verification | Select | Proving it dead is the one moment you are working on something that might be live. The label on the switch says what to wear; the meter goes nowhere without it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Live-dead-live | Sequence (strict order: known live source, switch lugs, known live source again) | A meter that reads zero can be a dead switch or a dead meter. Testing it on a known source before and after is the only way the zero means anything. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Make the cams ground-first | Sequence (strict order: ground (green), neutral (white), phase A, phase B, phase C) | Ground first so the frame is bonded before any conductor could be hot; neutral before phases so a load never sees a floating neutral. Off is the reverse: phases, neutral, ground last. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Strain-relieve the feeder | Select | Feeder is heavy and the crew walks on it. The strain relief takes the pull so the cams never do. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Cover the taps | Select | Nothing is energised with a bare lug exposed. The cover goes on before the switch goes on, every time. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Remove the lock and energise | Turn | Your lock, your key, your call. The area is clear because the first energisation is the moment a fault shows itself. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Read the phases at the distro | Graded reading | Three phases that read right at the distro is the proof the tie-in is correct. A lost neutral shows here first — as one phase high and one low — before it shows as a rack of dead dimmers. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Load test | Timed hold | Balanced under load is the last proof. A tie-in that reads right unloaded and sags under the first cue was never right. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Walk the feeder run | Find (any order: damaged feeder jacket) | Feeder lives on the floor with the whole load-in walking over it. The walk is what finds the cut that becomes a fault at the top of the show. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `phase-first` | You reached for a phase cam before the ground was on. Single-pole connectors are made ground first and broken ground last, so there is never a moment the distro frame is hot with no path back — that order is the whole reason cams are allowed. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `wet-distro` | The distro is standing in the puddle from the load-in. A metal frame carrying three phases on a wet floor is a shock path to whoever touches it next; it gets moved and dried before it gets power. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `taped-cam` | That cam is held in with tape because the latch is broken. A connector that can pull apart under load arcs — and a 400 A arc on a stage is a fire and a burn, not a fault. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `meter-no-gloves` | You went at the switch with the meter and no gloves or face shield. Verification is the live part of this job; it is done in the PPE the arc-flash label calls for, or it is not done. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Chain Hoist (SmartCiti.X · `chain-hoist`)

**Category:** Entertainment & Live Events  
**Trade:** Entertainment rigger — chain motors  
**Certification / standard claimed:** IATSE — ETCP Certified Rigger (Arena); ANSI E1.6-1 powered hoists; manufacturer chain-motor inspection and load-rating compliance  
**Tagline:** Flying a truss: load calc, chain inspection, bridle angle, moused hooks, test lift, deck clear, trim and lock  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the rigging plot | Select | The plot is the load, the points and the beams they hang from. Every number on it is checked before a rigger goes up. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Calculate the point load | Graded reading | The point load is the truss plus everything on it, divided across the points, with the bridle multiplying it. It is calculated, written down, and compared to the rating — not estimated. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Inspect each motor's load chain | Sequence (any order: motor 1 chain, motor 2 chain) | The chain is the only thing between the beam and the truss. A twist or a stretched link is found by hand, before the motor is under load. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Set the bridle angle | Graded reading | A wide bridle multiplies the load in each leg. At 120° each leg carries the full point load; the plot's angle is the one the legs were rated for. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Mouse the hooks | Sequence (any order: motor 1 hook, motor 2 hook) | Mousing keeps the sling in the hook through a bounce or a swing. It costs thirty seconds; a hook that walks off costs the truss. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Test lift | Timed hold | The test lift proves the points, the slings and the chain under the real load while the truss is still a few inches off the deck, where a failure is a bang, not a casualty. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Clear the deck | Select | The go is called by the person who has looked under the truss and heard clear from the deck. Not assumed, not from across the room. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Fly the truss to trim | Tracked hold | Motors that run at different speeds tilt the truss and shift load onto one point. The rigger watches the level and stops the fast one. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Mark the trim and lock the motors | Select | A locked controller cannot be bumped by a foot or a curious hand during the show. The trim mark is what the next call is checked against. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Walk the points before the house opens | Find (any order: shackle pin not seated) | Hardware is checked by eye and by hand after the fly, because the lift is what moves it. The walk is the last look before there are people underneath. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `under-truss` | You called the lift with a crew member under the truss. Nothing goes up over a body; the deck is cleared and confirmed before the go, every lift. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `kinked-chain` | You ran a motor with a kinked, twisted chain. A twist in the load chain jams in the hoist under load and the motor drops the point — that chain is tagged out, not run. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `hook-not-moused` | You lifted on a hook with no mousing. A hook without its latch or mousing can walk off the sling under a bounce; the point that unhooks is the point the truss swings on. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `over-rating` | You added the point-load to a motor already at its rated capacity. A one-tonne motor is a one-tonne motor; the fix is another point, not a hope. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Fly System (SmartCiti.X · `fly-system`)

**Category:** Entertainment & Live Events  
**Trade:** Theatrical rigger / fly operator  
**Certification / standard claimed:** IATSE — ETCP Certified Rigger (Theatre); ANSI E1.4-1 manual counterweight rigging systems; OSHA 29 CFR 1910.28 fall protection on the loading bridge; venue 'heads up' and lineset-tagging procedure  
**Tagline:** Counterweight lineset load: rail locked, deck cleared with a call, batten in, fixtures hung and safetied, loader clipped in on the bridge, bricks to match the pipe with spreaders and a lock ring, balanced test lift, trim, lock, clamp, tag  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the hang schedule | Select | The schedule says what goes on the pipe and what that weighs; the arbor is loaded to that number, not to how the rope feels. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Lock the rail | Select | Nothing is hung on a pipe that can move. The lock holds the lineset while it goes out of balance during loading; the operator is the second lock. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Call and clear the deck | Sequence (any order: 'heads up' call, deck clear under the batten) | The call is the only warning a stage has. Nobody stands under a pipe that is about to move, and the fly operator does not move it until the deck answers. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Bring the batten in | Tracked hold | Steady hand-over-hand keeps the lineset under control and the arbor from bouncing in its guides. A fast fly is a fly that gets away. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Hang and safety the fixtures | Sequence (strict order: fixtures clamped, safety cables, cable pick tied off) | A clamp holds a fixture until it does not; the safety cable is what holds it then. Cable ties come last so nothing is left hanging when the pipe flies. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Clip in on the loading bridge | Sequence (any order: harness clipped to the bridge line, bridge gate closed) | The loading bridge is a catwalk over the arbor pit forty feet up with an open side to load through. The tie-off is what keeps a loader who leans out on the bridge. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Load the arbor to the pipe | Graded reading | The arbor balances the pipe plus what is on it. Light, the pipe runs to the deck; heavy, the arbor runs to the loft. The number on the schedule is the number in the arbor. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Spreader plates and lock ring | Sequence (strict order: spreader plates, lock ring) | Spreaders keep the arbor rods from bowing under a stack; the ring keeps the stack from jumping the rods if the arbor stops hard. No ring, and the bricks leave the arbor on a runaway. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Test lift | Tracked hold | The test lift is the only proof of balance. A hand on the line feels a heavy pipe or a heavy arbor before it becomes a runaway; a foot of travel is enough to know. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Fly to trim | Graded reading | Trim is where the designer wants the light. It is set once, marked on the purchase line, and the clamp holds it for the run. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Lock, clamp, tag | Sequence (strict order: rope lock, trim clamp, lineset tag) | The tag tells the next crew what is on the pipe and in the arbor; the clamp holds trim; the lock holds everything. Nobody leaves an untagged lineset. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Walk the arbor before you leave the bridge | Find (any order: brick outside the rods) | The arbor is checked from the bridge before the loader unclips. A brick outside the rods is found now or on the deck. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `unlock-unbalanced` | You released the rope lock with the batten loaded and the arbor light. A pipe-heavy lineset runs away to the deck, the arbor flies to the loft, and the purchase line takes the hands off whoever is holding it. The lock stays on until the arbor matches the pipe. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `bridge-unclipped` | You stepped onto the loading bridge without clipping in. It is a forty-foot drop to the deck through a gap the width of an arbor, and the bricks you are handling weigh more than the harness. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `under-batten` | You walked under a batten that was moving. Everything on a moving pipe — a fixture, a safety cable, a wrench — lands on the deck under it; the stage is called clear and stays clear until the pipe stops. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `toss-brick` | You tossed a brick to the bridge instead of handing it. A counterweight brick is twenty pounds of steel; dropped from the bridge it goes through the deck, and through whoever is on it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

# Maritime & Ports

## Dock Crane (SmartCiti.X · `dock-crane`)

**Category:** Maritime & Ports  
**Trade:** Longshoreman / container-crane operator  
**Certification / standard claimed:** ILWU — OSHA 29 CFR 1917 qualified crane operator  
**Tagline:** Container lift: lashing release order, twist-lock verification and wind-limit discipline  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Hold the pre-lift briefing | Select | The lashing gang and the crane operator are working the same box from two different places — the briefing is what keeps their timing matched. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Check the load chart | Select | Rated capacity falls as boom radius increases. The chart is checked for this specific lift, not assumed from a similar box lifted yesterday. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Check wind speed against the rated limit | Graded reading | A container is a sail once it leaves the stack. Wind speed against the crane's rated limit is checked before the box is off the chassis, not discovered mid-swing. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Confirm the red zone is clear | Select | This is confirmed before the box leaves the stack — once it's swinging, the red zone is not a place anyone can duck into or out of safely. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Release the lashings top-down | Sequence (strict order: top-tier lashing rods, mid-tier lashing rods, bottom-tier lashing rods) | A stack is only stable while the tiers above are still tied. Releasing bottom-up leaves an upper tier held by nothing while someone is still standing among the containers. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Position the spreader over the container | Select | A spreader set even slightly off the castings will not seat the twist-locks properly no matter how solid they look going in. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Verify all four twist-locks | Find (any order: unlatched corner lock, corner light showing false-locked) | The panel tells you what the sensors think happened. Checking the handles is how you find out what actually happened. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Confirm with the signal person | Select | The operator's view of the container is partly blocked from the cab. The signal person is watching the one angle the operator can't. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Take the lift | Select | A smooth take-up lets you feel the load coming on evenly. A snatched lift is how a marginal lock finally lets go. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Land and release in order | Sequence (strict order: lower onto the chassis, release the twist-locks, retract the spreader) | The locks only release once the container's full weight is back on the chassis — releasing early hands the load's weight to whatever is left holding it, which may be nothing. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Teach the AGV route waypoints | Sequence (strict order: Pickup waypoint, Transit waypoint, Stack waypoint) | The AGV drives this route exactly in the order it was recorded — teach pickup, then transit, then stack, matching the path the container actually needs to travel. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Save the AGV route | Select | An untaught point list is just recorded positions. Saving it is what turns three waypoints into a route the AGV can actually dispatch on. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 13 | Dry-run the AGV route | Timed hold | A brand-new route is verified at walking pace with a hand on the console, watching the whole path, before an unmanned AGV ever drives it at full speed through a working yard. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 14 | Close the lift log | Select | The log is the record a surveyor or the next shift reads — what was lifted, in what wind, with which corners verified. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `under-load-zone` | You stepped into the red zone directly beneath a suspended container. A dropped box or a parted sling gives nobody standing under it any time to react — the red zone stays empty for the entire lift, not just while it looks like it's moving. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `lashing-cutter` | That is a cutting tool, not a release. Cutting a lashing rod instead of unwinding the turnbuckle releases it with no control at all — exactly the uncontrolled shift that the top-down order exists to prevent. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `hoist-override` | That switch silences the unseated twist-lock warning instead of fixing it. Hoisting on an overridden warning means you find out which corner wasn't locked only after the container is already in the air. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `wind-override` | That switch bypasses the wind-speed lockout. Above the crane's rated wind limit the load can swing further than the operator can control, and overriding the limit does not change what the wind is actually doing to the box. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Container Lashing (SmartCiti.X · `container-lashing`)

**Category:** Maritime & Ports  
**Trade:** Longshore worker — lasher  
**Certification / standard claimed:** ILWU — OSHA 29 CFR 1918 (longshoring) marine terminal safety; ship's Cargo Securing Manual (IMO CSS Code) lashing pattern; fall protection on lashing bridges  
**Tagline:** Deck stow securing: crane held off, twist-locks proven, rods and turnbuckles to the pattern, fall protection on the bridge, torque checked  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the bay plan and securing pattern | Select | The pattern depends on the stack: how many tiers, how heavy, where on deck. The manual decides which castings get rods — not whichever rods are closest. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Hold the crane off the bay | Select | The crane and the lashers never share a bay. The hold goes on the radio before the first lasher steps onto the deck, and comes off from the same radio when the last one is out. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Harness on, clipped to the bridge line | Sequence (any order: harness, lanyard clipped to the bridge line) | The bridge is the workplace three tiers up. Clipped in, a slip is a scare; unclipped, it is a fall onto steel or into the bay. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Prove every twist-lock | Sequence (any order: fore-port lock, fore-starboard lock, aft-port lock, aft-starboard lock) | A twist-lock in the unlocked position looks exactly like a locked one from the bridge. Four corners, by hand, is the only proof — and the reason the lashers are here at all. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Fit the lashing rods to the pattern | Sequence (strict order: port lashing rod, starboard lashing rod) | Rods take the racking load when the ship rolls. Crossed, port and starboard, they hold the stack against both directions; one side alone holds against one. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Seat the rod in the casting | Timed hold | A rod that is hooked but not seated pulls out on the first roll. It is held in until it seats — you feel it drop. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Hand-tighten the turnbuckles | Sequence (strict order: port turnbuckle, starboard turnbuckle) | Even take-up on both sides keeps the stack square. One side cranked tight before the other pulls the stack over toward it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Set the turnbuckle tension | Graded reading | Under-tensioned lashings go slack on the first roll; over-tensioned ones pre-load the castings. The manual's figure is the figure. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Lock the turnbuckles | Turn | A turnbuckle without its lock nut works loose with every roll. The nut is what keeps the tension you just set. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Walk the bay before the all-clear | Find (any order: twist-lock left unlocked on the upper tier) | The walk-out is the last time anyone sees this bay before the ship sails. What you do not catch here, the sea does. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Give the crane the all-clear | Select | The hold comes off from the same radio it went on, by the person who put it on, when every lasher is out. Never assumed, never relayed. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `under-load` | You walked under the spreader with a box on it. Nothing goes under a suspended container — a twist-lock that let go on the last lift is the one you find out about from underneath. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `untied-bridge` | You went onto the lashing bridge without clipping in. It is a narrow steel walkway three tiers up over open deck; the harness is worn, and it is clipped, or the bridge is not worked. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `hand-on-cone` | You put your hand on a twist-lock while the box is still coming down. Fingers between a 30-tonne container and its corner casting are gone before you feel the pinch. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `bent-rod` | You went to fit a lashing rod with a bent shank. A rod that is not straight does not seat in the casting and does not take the load the pattern assumes — it goes to the reject rack, not on the stack. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Mooring Line (SmartCiti.X · `mooring-line`)

**Category:** Maritime & Ports  
**Trade:** Line handler / wharf mooring crew  
**Certification / standard claimed:** ILWU / SIU — OSHA 29 CFR 1917 marine terminals (mooring operations); OCIMF Mooring Equipment Guidelines snap-back awareness; port authority line-handling qualification  
**Tagline:** Taking a ship's lines: snap-back zone, heaving line, eye on the bollard, tension by radio, stopper, and the bight you never stand in  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the mooring plan | Select | The plan is the ship's, agreed with the wharf. It says where each line goes and in what order, so the handlers are at the right bollard before the line arrives. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Hi-vis, life vest, gloves and boots | Sequence (any order: hi-vis, life vest, gloves and boots) | A line handler works at the wharf edge, at night, under a ship's lights. Seen, floating and gripping is the minimum. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Identify the snap-back zone | Select | The zone is where a parted line goes. It is marked so the decision is made before the line is under load, not during. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Establish comms with the ship | Select | The winch is on the ship and the bollard is on the wharf. Everything between them is a radio call — nobody on the wharf guesses when the ship will heave. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Take the heaving line | Select | The heaving line is how a hundred metres of mooring line crosses the gap. The fist is weighted to fly; it is picked up off the deck, never caught. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Haul the mooring line ashore | Tracked hold | Steady hauling keeps the eye coming; stopping lets the line drop into the water and drift under the ship. Hands stay on the line, feet stay out of every loop it makes on the wharf. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Drop the eye on the bollard | Carry and place | The eye goes over the bollard from the outside, and the hands come off before the ship takes any weight. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Call the tension | Graded reading | The ship cannot see the line at the bollard. The handler calls when it is fair and tight — before it is bar-taut, which is the tension a line parts at. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Hold the stopper for the transfer | Timed hold | The stopper holds the load while the ship makes the line fast on board. Let it go early and the line runs — with your hands near it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Walk the lines before all fast | Find (any order: chafed line at the fairlead) | A line parts where it is weakest, and the wharf handler is the only one who sees the wharf end. The walk is the last look before the ship is all fast. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `in-bight` | You stood inside the bight of the line on the wharf. When the winch takes up, the bight closes on whatever is inside it — a leg, at a speed no one steps out of. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `snap-back-zone` | You stood in line with the tensioned mooring line. When a line parts, it whips back through that zone faster than you can see it; the zone is painted on the wharf so nobody has to guess where not to stand. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `hand-on-bollard` | You put your hand on the bollard while the eye was being slacked. Fingers between the eye and the bollard are gone the moment the ship takes up. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `heaving-line-catch` | You went to catch the heaving line's monkey's fist in the air. It is weighted; you let it land, then pick it up — a caught fist is a broken hand. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Bunkering Watch (SmartCiti.X · `bunkering-watch`)

**Category:** Maritime & Ports  
**Trade:** Marine engineer — person in charge of oil transfer  
**Certification / standard claimed:** MEBA / SIU / MM&P — USCG 33 CFR 155.710 person in charge of oil transfer; 33 CFR 156.150 declaration of inspection; MARPOL Annex VI bunker delivery note and sample (Reg. 18); ISGOTT ship/barge bunkering checklist; STCW A-VI/1  
**Tagline:** Fuel-oil transfer: plan and signed DOI, scuppers plugged and drip tray and SOPEP kit set, flange bolted all round, radio and emergency stop tested, line-up tank-first, slow start, soundings, topping off at reduced rate, manifold closed, sample sealed, BDN signed  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the bunker plan | Select | The plan is how much goes where and in what order so the ship stays in trim and no tank is filled past 90 percent. Everything the barge does is measured against it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Sign the declaration of inspection | Select | The DOI is the law's checklist: both persons in charge confirm the hose, the connections, the comms, the signals, the emergency stop and the quantity before a drop moves. It is signed, not assumed. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Contain the deck | Sequence (any order: scupper plugs, manifold drip tray, SOPEP kit staged) | The deck is the containment. Plugged scuppers keep a spill on board; the tray takes the drips a flange always makes; the kit is what turns a spill into a mop-up instead of a report. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Connect the hose | Sequence (strict order: hose flange landed, every bolt hole filled, unused manifold blanked) | Half the bolts hold a flange until the pump surges. The unused manifold is blanked because a valve is not a blank, and a valve left cracked open on an unblanked branch is a spill nobody sees start. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Prove comms and the emergency stop | Sequence (any order: radio check with the barge, emergency stop tested) | The one thing that must work during a transfer is 'stop'. It is tested before the pump starts, because testing it after is a spill. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Line up tank-first | Sequence (strict order: receiving tank valve, manifold valve) | The line is opened from the tank back to the manifold so there is always somewhere for the oil to go. A manifold opened onto a closed tank valve is a hose at pump pressure with no outlet. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Slow start and walk the deck | Tracked hold | The first minutes are at a rate a drip tray can hold. The full rate is agreed only after the whole line has been seen under pressure and is dry. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Take a sounding | Graded reading | The barge's meter is the barge's number. The ship's number is the sounding, taken by hand, and the two are compared every fifteen minutes. A sounding that does not match the plan is a stop. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Top off at reduced rate | Graded reading | Topping off is the overflow hazard. The rate comes down so a sounding every minute stays ahead of the tank, and the tank stops at 90 percent, never full. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Close the manifold | Turn | The line is blown clear before the manifold closes so the hose is drained back to the barge and not onto the deck at disconnection. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Seal the sample and sign the BDN | Sequence (strict order: continuous drip sample, sample sealed and labelled, bunker delivery note signed) | The MARPOL sample is the ship's evidence of what it was sold; the BDN is the receipt. Both are kept aboard for a year and inspected at the next port. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 12 | Walk the deck before the hose comes off | Find (any order: dislodged scupper plug) | The disconnection is the last chance to spill. The deck is walked again before the flange is broken, because a plug that walked out during the transfer is found now or on the water. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `start-uncontained` | You started the transfer with a scupper open. The first thing a hose does when a flange weeps is put oil on the deck, and an open scupper puts that oil in the harbour: a reportable spill, a detained ship and a cleanup bill by the tonne. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `no-doi` | You started pumping because the barge said 'go'. The declaration of inspection is the two persons in charge agreeing on the tanks, the quantity, the rate, the signals and the stop — without it, nobody has agreed on anything. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `topping-full-rate` | You topped off at full rate. A tank that is 90 percent full takes two minutes to overflow at the transfer rate; the vent goes first, then the deck, then the water. Topping off is done slow, sounding every minute. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `phone-on-deck` | You used a phone on the tank deck during transfer. Vents are breathing fuel vapour a few metres from you; only intrinsically safe equipment is allowed in the hazardous zone while bunkering. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

# Environmental Monitoring

## Hunters Point Briefing (SmartCiti.X · `hunters-point`)

**Category:** Environmental Monitoring  
**Trade:** Environmental monitoring technician  
**Certification / standard claimed:** LIUNA hazmat & environmental laborer — OSHA HAZWOPER (29 CFR 1910.120) 40-hour with annual refresher; chain-of-custody sampling under EPA QA/QC guidance  
**Tagline:** A real Superfund site, a real data-fraud case, a real community air-monitoring effort — flat briefing and knowledge check, not a walkable scene  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 10/10 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

_Flat briefing station: a sourced dossier and knowledge check, no walkable scene._

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | What is Hunters Point Naval Shipyard today? | Select | The site's legal status sets every rule for anyone working near it. It has been on the National Priorities List since 1989, the Navy is the lead agency, and the EPA and the state regulators oversee the Navy — that is who signs off on whether a parcel is done. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Which contaminants does the record list at the site? | Sequence (any order: radionuclides, PCBs, heavy metals, petroleum fuels) | What is in the ground decides the PPE, the monitoring, the disposal route and the clearance criteria. A crew that thinks it is dealing with one contaminant plans for one and is exposed to the rest. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | What did the contractor data case establish? | Select | The scale is the point: when roughly half a contractor's radiological data is suspect, every clearance built on it is in question. The whole discipline of environmental monitoring exists so that a number can be trusted — and this case is what it looks like when it can't. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | What actually protects data integrity on a monitoring crew? | Select | More samples from the same untrusted process are just more untrusted numbers. Chain of custody ties a result to a place and a person; split samples and an independent lab make a fabricated result disagree with something. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Who oversees the cleanup? | Select | Knowing who signs what tells you whose data you are producing and who will audit it. A contractor certifying its own work is precisely the failure this site is known for. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Where does independent neighborhood air data come from? | Select | On a site where the official record was found unreliable, an independent measurement is the control, not a courtesy. The community effort is real, named, and ongoing — and it is not something this simulator runs or speaks for. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Which trades does the actual work call on? | Sequence (any order: hazmat laborers (LIUNA), operating engineers (IUOE), Teamsters hauling regulated soil, radiation control technicians) | Environmental monitoring is not one job. Excavation, hauling, survey, sampling and hygiene are separate trades with separate training gates, and the crew is only as safe as the least-trained person inside the fence. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | What is the minimum training for a hazmat laborer here? | Select | HAZWOPER is the federal floor for anyone who could be exposed to hazardous substances on an uncontrolled site. The 40-hour course, the supervised days and the annual refresher are the rule — site-specific training sits on top of it, never instead of it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | A perimeter dust monitor alarms during excavation. What now? | Select | The perimeter monitor exists for the neighborhood on the other side of the fence. Stop, control the source, notify — the excavator restarts when the air says so, not when the schedule does. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Where does the record stand? | Select | Anyone briefing a crew on this site has to say honestly that it is unfinished: parcels still under cleanup, litigation still open, a neighborhood still measuring its own air. That is the frame you work inside — not a story with an ending. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `walk-in` | You treated an active Superfund parcel as open ground. Parcels here are behind fences and institutional controls for a reason: residual contamination, ongoing excavation and radiological survey work. Site access is by the Navy's controls and a HAZWOPER-trained escort, never on your own read of a map. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `swap-sample` | You chose to swap in a sample that reads clean. That is exactly what the False Claims Act case was about: substituted soil and fabricated readings that a whole cleanup was then judged on. A sample is the location it came from, logged under chain of custody, or it is nothing. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `silence-alarm` | You silenced the perimeter dust monitor to keep the excavator moving. The monitor is the neighborhood's only real-time protection from what the dig is lifting; the response to an alarm is stop, wet down, notify — not mute. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `dismiss-community` | You dismissed the community's air monitors as noise. Independent neighborhood data is a check on the site's own record — on this site in particular, where the contractor's data was found unreliable, an outside measurement is not a nuisance, it is the control. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Dossier sources

- **What the site is** — EPA Superfund site profile <https://cumulis.epa.gov/supercpad/cursites/csitinfo.cfm?id=0902722>; SF.gov — HPNS cleanup <https://www.sf.gov/hpns-cleanup-learn>
- **The data-integrity case** — U.S. Department of Justice press release (2026) <https://www.justice.gov/opa/pr/tetra-tech-ec-inc-agrees-pay-57m-settle-false-claims-act-allegations-falsifying-soil-test>
- **The community and the litigation** — Greenaction — Bayview Hunters Point <https://greenaction.org/bayview-hunters-point/>; Greenaction — in memory of Marie Harrison <https://greenaction.org/2020/05/06/in-honor-and-memory-of-marie-harrison-1-30-1948-5-5-2019/>
- **Who does the work** — OSHA 29 CFR 1910.120 — HAZWOPER <https://www.osha.gov/laws-regs/regulations/standardnumber/1910/1910.120>

## Perimeter Air (SmartCiti.X · `air-monitor`)

**Category:** Environmental Monitoring  
**Trade:** Environmental monitoring technician  
**Certification / standard claimed:** LIUNA hazmat & environmental laborer — OSHA HAZWOPER 40-hour (29 CFR 1910.120); perimeter air monitoring under a site-specific Air Monitoring Plan required by the EPA / state cleanup order  
**Tagline:** Real-time PM10 fence-line monitoring: wind, placement, flow calibration, zero check, and the exceedance response  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the Air Monitoring Plan | Select | The plan says how many monitors, where relative to the wind, what PM10 level triggers action and what that action is. Everything on the line follows from it — you read it first, not when the alarm goes. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Check the wind | Select | Upwind gives you background; downwind gives you what the site adds. Without the wind, a monitor is a box that measures somewhere. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Place the monitors by the wind | Sequence (strict order: upwind monitor, downwind monitor) | The upwind reading is the number every downwind reading is judged against. It goes in first so the pair is logging together from the first minute of the dig. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Calibrate the sampler flow | Graded reading | A PM10 inlet only cuts at 10 microns at its design flow. Off-flow, the size cut moves and the number the plan compares to its action level is a different quantity. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Run the zero check | Timed hold | A monitor that will not read zero on filtered air cannot be trusted at the action level. The zero is the proof the instrument, not the site, is what you are measuring. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Start the logger with synced time | Select | An exceedance is a time, a wind and a reading together. Two loggers a minute apart are two stories; synced clocks are one record. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Raise and lock the telemetry mast | Turn | The monitor beside the fence is only useful if someone sees it when it alarms. Telemetry puts the reading in the trailer and on the health and safety officer's phone. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Walk the line and find the fault | Find (any order: blocked inlet) | Calibration proves the flow through a clean inlet. A blocked screen fails quietly, low, on the downwind side — the walk-down is what catches it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Answer the exceedance | Sequence (strict order: stop the excavator, wet the work face, notify the health and safety officer) | Stop removes the source, water holds it down, the notification starts the record and the decision on restart. The order is the plan's; the mute button is not in it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Log the exceedance | Select | The regulators, the neighbours and the next shift all read the same log. What was measured, what was done and when — written while it is true, not remembered later. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `alarm-mute` | You muted the monitor. The alarm is the plan's trigger — mute it and the excavator keeps lifting dust across a fence line with houses on the other side, and the record shows a technician chose that. Stop, wet, notify; the mute button is not a response. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `monitor-in-exhaust` | You set the monitor beside the generator exhaust. It will read the engine, not the site — a false high shuts the job for nothing, and worse, a monitor you learn to distrust is one nobody acts on when it is right. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `zone-no-respirator` | You walked into the exclusion zone without your respirator on. The perimeter instruments are for the neighbours; inside the fence, the protection is what is on your face, and the monitors will not warn you in time. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `refuel-running` | You went to refuel the generator while it was running. Fuel on a hot engine beside the instruments is a fire, and the plume of a fire is the one exceedance the plan cannot answer. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Automated preview (machine checks, not a practitioner verdict)

| Check | Result | Note |

## Sampling Well (SmartCiti.X · `sampling-well`)

**Category:** Environmental Monitoring  
**Trade:** Environmental sampling technician  
**Certification / standard claimed:** LIUNA hazmat & environmental laborer — OSHA HAZWOPER 40-hour (29 CFR 1910.120); low-flow groundwater sampling per the EPA Region 4 SESD operating procedure; chain of custody under the site QAPP (EPA QA/G-5)  
**Tagline:** Low-flow groundwater sampling: water level, pump set, stabilised purge, volatiles first, chain of custody  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the sampling plan | Select | The plan names the well, what the lab will analyse, which bottle fills first and how long each has before it is worthless. You read it here, not at the cooler. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Gloves and glasses | Sequence (any order: nitrile gloves, safety glasses) | The gloves protect you from the water and the water from you — a fingerprint of yesterday's well is cross-contamination. New pair, every well. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Screen the casing headspace | Select | The casing can hold vapour. The meter goes to the opening first; the reading tells you whether this is a routine well or a respirator well. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Measure the water level | Graded reading | Depth to water sets where the intake goes and is the baseline drawdown is measured against. It is the first number on the field sheet because everything after depends on it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Set the pump in the screen | Carry and place | The intake sits in the screen, mid-interval, so what you draw is formation water at that depth — not casing water above it, not sediment below it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Purge at low flow | Tracked hold | Low flow is the whole method: a rate the well can supply without drawdown, so the sample is the aquifer moving through the screen. Too fast pulls the column down and stirs it; too slow and the parameters never stabilise. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Confirm the parameters have stabilised | Sequence (any order: pH, conductivity, dissolved oxygen) | Stable parameters are the evidence the stagnant casing water is gone and formation water is at the pump. The bottles wait for it; a sample filled before stabilisation is a sample of the well, not the aquifer. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Fill the bottles in order | Sequence (strict order: VOC vials, metals bottle, general chemistry bottle) | Volatiles leave the water every second it is exposed, so they are captured first, gently, with no air in the vial. The order is the analytes' order of fragility, not the label order. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Sign the chain of custody | Select | From here the bottle is evidence. The form is the unbroken line from this casing to the lab bench, and every hand it passes through signs it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Pack on ice | Timed hold | Most holding times assume the sample is at 4 °C from the minute it is taken. Warm on the tailgate for an hour and the metals are fine and the volatiles are gone. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Check the labels before the truck moves | Find (any order: mislabelled bottle) | The lab trusts the label. A mismatch caught here is a pen stroke; caught at the bench, it is a resample and a report nobody can use. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `bailer` | You reached for the bailer. Dropping a bailer down the well agitates the column, strips volatiles and stirs sediment — the sample would be of the disturbance, not the aquifer. Low-flow means the pump, set in the screen, at a rate the well can give. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `purge-to-ground` | You dumped the purge water on the ground. It is groundwater from a contaminated site: it goes into the labelled purge drum for characterisation and disposal, not back into the soil beside the well you are trying to characterise. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `head-over-well` | You leaned your head over the open casing. A well on this site can carry hydrogen sulfide or VOC vapour at the casing; you read the meter at the opening before your face is anywhere near it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `truck-idling` | The truck is idling upwind of the open bottles. Engine exhaust is a volatile organic source — it can put a detection in a VOC vial that the aquifer never had, and a false detection here is a false plume. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Stormwater Outfall (SmartCiti.X · `stormwater-outfall`)

**Category:** Environmental Monitoring  
**Trade:** Environmental sampling technician — stormwater  
**Certification / standard claimed:** Clean Water Act NPDES industrial stormwater permit (first-flush grab within 30 minutes of discharge); 40 CFR 136 approved methods, preservation and hold times; chain-of-custody per EPA SESD; OSHA HAZWOPER awareness and 29 CFR 1910.146 for confined vault access  
**Tagline:** Wet-weather NPDES grab: permit window, meters calibrated before the rain, bank staged with a buddy and tripod, grab from the flow, bottles in preservation order, field readings logged, custody sealed, sample on ice  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the permit's sampling requirement | Select | The permit says what a qualifying storm is, how long you have after discharge starts, which bottles and preservatives, and how long the lab has. Miss the window and the quarter has no sample. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Confirm the storm qualifies | Select | A qualifying event needs enough rain after enough dry days. Sampling a storm that does not qualify wastes the bottles and still leaves the permit unsatisfied. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Calibrate the field meters | Sequence (strict order: pH 4 buffer, pH 7 buffer, conductivity standard) | Field readings are data of record. A meter calibrated after the sample, or not at all, turns every number on the form into an estimate the regulator can strike. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Stage the bank | Sequence (any order: second person on the bank, tripod and line, nitrile gloves) | The bank is wet, steep and moving. The tripod keeps the sampler over the flow without anyone leaning out, and the gloves keep the site's chemistry out of the bottle and off the technician. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Estimate the flow | Graded reading | A concentration without a flow is half a number. The stage reading is what turns milligrams per litre into the pounds per day the permit is actually written against. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Take the grab from the flow | Timed hold | The sample is taken from the flow, not from the bank eddy where solids settle and oil collects. Mouth upstream so what goes in is what is running past. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Fill the bottles in preservation order | Sequence (strict order: oil and grease (no headspace), metals (nitric acid), BOD / TSS (unpreserved)) | Oil and grease is a bottle you never pour between, so it is taken first and directly. The acid bottle is filled before anything can carry acid into a bottle that must not have it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Read field pH | Graded reading | pH has a fifteen-minute hold time: it is a field parameter or it is nothing. It is read on an aliquot, never in the sample bottle going to the lab. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Label, seal and sign custody | Sequence (strict order: labels with time and site, custody seals, chain of custody signed) | Custody is what makes the sample evidence. Every hand that holds it signs for it, and a broken seal at the lab ends the sample's life as a legal record. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Get the samples on ice | Carry and place | Four degrees is part of the method. A metals sample survives, but the unpreserved bottles start changing the moment they are warm, and the lab reports the sample as out of compliance on arrival. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Walk the outfall before you leave | Find (any order: sheen on the upstream water) | The sampler is the only person who sees the outfall during the storm. What is visible in the flow is reportable the same day, not when the lab report comes back in three weeks. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `wade-the-channel` | You waded into the channel to reach the flow. Six inches of moving stormwater takes a person off their feet, the outfall is a culvert, and nobody on the bank can reach you once you are in it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `bare-hands-sample` | You handled the sample with bare hands. Industrial stormwater carries the site's chemistry; bare skin contaminates the sample with your own and exposes you to whatever washed off the yard. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `vault-entry-alone` | You opened the junction vault and leaned in alone. A stormwater vault is a permit-required confined space: it collects heavier-than-air vapour off the yard and it has drowned people in a metre of water. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `unlabelled-bottle` | You filled a bottle with no label and no time. A sample without a label, a time and a signature is not evidence of anything; the lab will reject it and the permit sample is gone with the rain. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

# Trade Skills Simulator

## Isolation Bay (Trade Skills · `electrical`)

**Category:** Trade Skills Simulator  
**Trade:** Electrical worker  
**Union:** IBEW — International Brotherhood of Electrical Workers (inside wireman JATC)  
**Certification / standard claimed:** NFPA 70E electrical safety in the workplace; OSHA 29 CFR 1910.147 control of hazardous energy (lockout/tagout) and 1910.333 work practices; IBEW/NECA JATC electrical safety training  
**Tagline:** Lockout/tagout and absence-of-voltage verification on a 480 V distribution panel  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Don arc-rated PPE | Select | Shock and arc-flash protection go on before you enter the approach boundary, not once the door is open. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Identify the circuit | Select | The schedule tells you what the breaker feeds — and what else goes dark when you open it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Notify affected workers | Select | Affected employees must be told before and after isolation. An unannounced outage is how someone re-energises your circuit. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Open the disconnect | Select | Opening the disconnect is the isolation itself. Everything after this verifies and protects it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Apply your lock and tag | Select | Your lock, your key, your control. The tag names who applied it and why, so nobody guesses. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Prove the meter — live | Graded reading | A meter that reads zero because it is broken looks exactly like a dead circuit. Prove it works first. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Test for absence of voltage | Sequence (any order: L1 phase conductor, L2 phase conductor, L3 phase conductor) | Absence of voltage means every conductor you could contact, not the one you expect to be dead. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Re-prove the meter — live | Graded reading | The meter can fail between the dead test and now. Live-dead-live is one instrument check, not two halves. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Apply temporary protective grounds | Select | Grounds hold the conductors at earth potential if the circuit is ever back-fed while you are inside it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `live-bus` | You reached into an energised enclosure. Exposed bus at 480 V will not warn you first — nothing enters that cabinet until the disconnect is open, locked, and proven dead. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `foreign-lock` | That padlock belongs to another worker. You never remove someone else's lock or tag: their life is on the other end of it. Each worker applies and removes their own. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `damaged-leads` | Those test leads have cracked insulation at the strain relief. Damaged leads put your hand at line potential — bin them and draw a rated set. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Colour Studio (Trade Skills · `salon`)

**Category:** Trade Skills Simulator  
**Trade:** Hair stylist / colourist  
**Union:** Licensed trade under the state board of barbering and cosmetology (non-union in most states; some salon locals organise under UFCW)  
**Certification / standard claimed:** State cosmetology licence; state board sanitation and disinfection rules (EPA-registered hospital-grade disinfectant, one-use implements); OSHA Hazard Communication (29 CFR 1910.1200) for colour and developer SDS  
**Tagline:** Oxidative colour service from consultation to rinse, with station sanitation  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Consult and check the patch test | Select | The patch test is done 48 hours ahead and the record proves it. No record, no oxidative colour — and previous services decide whether the hair can take this one. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Disinfect the tools | Select | Tools are cleaned then fully immersed in an approved disinfectant between clients. Contact time is what does the work, so they go in before you need them. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Drape the client | Select | The drape protects skin and clothing, and the towel stops dye running onto the neck where it stains and sensitises. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Glove up | Select | Gloves go on before mixing, not before applying. The bowl is the first point of contact. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Mix to the right developer volume | Graded reading | Developer volume sets the lift: 20 vol gives roughly two levels and grey coverage. Reaching for a higher volume to go faster buys damage, not time. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Section into four quadrants | Sequence (any order: left front quadrant, right front quadrant, left nape quadrant, right nape quadrant) | Four quadrants give you control of saturation and timing. Applying into unsectioned hair is how you get patchy roots and missed grey. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Apply root to mid-length | Select | Regrowth first: the root is warmest from scalp heat and processes fastest, so it gets product first and the mid-lengths follow. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Process and pull on time | Graded reading | Development is a chemical clock. Pulled early the colour is under-deposited; left long the cuticle keeps swelling and the hair goes porous. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Rinse and neutralise | Select | Rinsing stops the reaction. Residual oxidant keeps working in the strand and irritates the scalp long after the client leaves. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Strip down the station | Select | Mixed colour is chemical waste and gloves are contaminated. The next client's service starts with a clean station or it starts compromised. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `metal-bowl` | That is a metal mixing bowl. Metal reacts with peroxide developer — it degrades the oxidant and can turn the formula. Colour is mixed in a non-metallic bowl, every time. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `dirty-shears` | Those shears came straight off the last client. Tools are cleaned and immersed in disinfectant between every client — a nicked scalp turns cross-contamination into a blood-borne exposure. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `flat-iron` | The iron is at 200 °C and the hair is saturated with product. Thermal styling on wet, chemically processed hair boils water inside the strand and snaps it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
