## Hot Line (Trade Skills · `kitchen`)

**Category:** Trade Skills Simulator  
**Trade:** Commercial cook  
**Union:** UNITE HERE — hospitality and food service workers  
**Certification / standard claimed:** ANSI-accredited food handler card and ServSafe Food Protection Manager (FDA Food Code: hand hygiene, cross-contamination, cook temperatures); OSHA 29 CFR 1910.157 portable extinguishers (Class K) for grease fires  
**Tagline:** Hand hygiene, cross-contamination control, cook temperature and a grease flare-up  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Wash hands — full 20 seconds | Timed hold | Twenty seconds of friction is the whole point; a three-second rinse moves contamination around instead of removing it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Take the raw-poultry board | Select | Red is raw meat and poultry. Colour coding exists so the decision is visible from across the line, not remembered. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Sanitise the board and surface | Select | Clean removes soil, sanitise reduces pathogens. A board that looks clean has not been sanitised. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Draw a knife from the rack | Select | Knives live on the rack or in your hand — never loose in a sink or under a towel where somebody finds the edge. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Portion to an even thickness | Graded reading | Even portions cook evenly. Ragged thickness means the thin end is dry before the thick end is safe. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Cook on the flat top | Select | The protein goes straight from board to heat — nothing sits at room temperature in the danger zone waiting for a burner. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Probe the internal temperature | Graded reading | Poultry is done at 74 °C / 165 °F in the thickest part. Colour is not a doneness test — the probe is. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Cut the heat | Select | Fuel first. Smothering a burner that is still firing just feeds the flame from underneath. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Smother the flare-up | Select | Starve it of oxygen and leave the lid on until it cools. Lifting the lid to look re-ignites it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Move to hot holding | Select | Hot holding stays at or above 135 °F. Below that you are incubating what the cook step just killed. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `green-board` | That is the produce board. Raw poultry on a board that goes back to salad is textbook cross-contamination — the pathogen does not care that you rinsed it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `water-jug` | Never water on a grease fire. Water flashes to steam under burning oil and throws the whole pan across the line. Cut the heat and smother it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `sink-knife` | There is a knife under the water in that sink. Blades never go into a full sink — the next person reaching in finds it with their hand. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `salad-station` | Those are ready-to-eat greens and you still have raw chicken on your hands. Change gloves and wash before you cross from raw to ready. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `extinguisher-a` | That is a water-based extinguisher. On burning oil it does exactly what the water jug does. The kitchen unit is the wet chemical Class K on the other wall. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Draw Station (Trade Skills · `phlebotomy`)

**Category:** Trade Skills Simulator  
**Trade:** Phlebotomy technician  
**Union:** SEIU / NUHW — healthcare workers (hospital and laboratory locals)  
**Certification / standard claimed:** NHA CPT or ASCP PBT phlebotomy technician certification; CLSI GP41 venipuncture standard (two-identifier check, order of draw); OSHA Bloodborne Pathogens (29 CFR 1910.1030)  
**Tagline:** Two-identifier check, venipuncture technique and the order of draw  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Verify two patient identifiers | Select | Two identifiers, actively confirmed. Room number and 'the patient said yes' are not identifiers. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Hand hygiene and gloves | Select | Gloves protect both directions. They go on after hand hygiene, not instead of it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Apply the tourniquet | Select | It stays on for no more than a minute. Longer and you get haemoconcentration — the results shift before the needle is even in. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Clean the site and let it dry | Select | Wet alcohol stings on entry and haemolyses the sample. Drying time is part of the antisepsis, not a delay. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Insert bevel up at the right angle | Graded reading | Fifteen to thirty degrees, bevel up. Too shallow and you skate off the vein; too steep and you go straight through it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Fill tubes in the order of draw | Sequence (strict order: blood culture, light blue — citrate, gold — SST, green — heparin, lavender — EDTA, grey — fluoride) | Additive carries over from tube to tube. EDTA before a chemistry tube throws potassium and calcium; citrate ratios break if the blue tube is not early and full. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Release the tourniquet | Select | Withdrawing under pressure forces a haematoma. The tourniquet comes off first, then the needle. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Activate the safety device and dispose | Select | The device is activated before your hand leaves the needle, and it goes into sharps immediately — never onto the tray. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Hold pressure on the site | Timed hold | Direct pressure until bleeding stops. Bending the elbow over the gauze is what produces the bruise the patient remembers you by. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Label the tubes at the bedside | Select | Labelling happens at the bedside with the patient present. Every step away from the chair is a chance for the sample to become someone else's. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `recap` | Never recap a used needle two-handed. That is the classic needlestick — activate the safety device and drop it straight into sharps. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `sharps-full` | That container is past the fill line. Forcing another sharp in is how the one already inside comes back out at your hand. Seal it and start a fresh one. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `iv-arm` | There is an infusion running in that arm. Drawing above an IV dilutes the sample with whatever is in the bag — wrong arm, wrong result. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `pre-labelled` | Those tubes were labelled before the draw. Pre-labelling is how samples get attributed to the wrong patient; tubes are labelled at the bedside, after they are filled. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Weld Bay (Trade Skills · `welding`)

**Category:** Trade Skills Simulator  
**Trade:** Welder / fabricator  
**Union:** Boilermakers (IBB), Ironworkers and UA welders; AWS Certified Welder programme  
**Certification / standard claimed:** AWS D1.1 welder performance qualification; OSHA 29 CFR 1910.252 welding, cutting and brazing; NFPA 51B hot work permit and fire watch; ANSI Z49.1 fume control and lens shade  
**Tagline:** Hot work permit, fume extraction, lens shade, bead control and fire watch  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Check the hot work permit | Select | The permit is the agreement that this area was surveyed and someone owns the watch. No permit, no arc. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Clear combustibles from the radius | Select | Combustibles go out to the required radius, or get covered. Sparks roll a long way across a shop floor. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Cover what cannot be moved | Select | Anything that cannot leave gets shielded. A blanket over the run is what stops a spark lodging in it unnoticed. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Position the fume extraction | Select | Extraction is positioned at the fume, not near it. Manganese and hexavalent chromium do their damage over a career, quietly. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Put on the full kit | Sequence (any order: welding helmet, leather jacket, welding gloves) | Arc burns skin like sun at close range and spatter goes everywhere. Partial PPE is the burn you explain later. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Set the lens shade | Graded reading | Too light and the arc reaches your eyes; too dark and you cannot see the puddle, so you chase it and ruin the bead. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Attach the work clamp | Select | The return path goes on the work, close to the weld. Letting the current find its own way through the bench and the building is how bearings and hinges get destroyed. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Set the amperage | Graded reading | Amperage follows electrode diameter and plate thickness. Too low and it sticks; too high and you burn through and undercut the toes. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Run the bead | Graded reading | Steady travel and a short arc length build an even bead. Racing gives you a thin ropey cap; crawling piles it up and traps slag. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Hold the fire watch | Timed hold | The watch continues after the arc stops, because that is when smouldering finds its way into something. Walking away at the last bead is how a shop burns down at 2 am. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `solvent-drum` | There is an open solvent drum inside the hot work radius. Sparks travel further than people think and vapour ignites well below the flash you can see — it leaves the area or the work does. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `cylinder-loose` | That cylinder is lying unsecured with the cap off. A knocked-over cylinder with a sheared valve becomes a projectile — upright, chained, capped when not in use. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `wet-gloves` | Those gloves are damp. Wet leather conducts, and SMAW open-circuit voltage does not need much help to find a path through you. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `grinder-noguard` | The guard is off that grinder. A disc that lets go at full speed goes straight into whatever is in line with it, and that is usually your hand or your face. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `arc-bare` | You looked at the arc without the helmet down. Arc eye is a burn to the cornea and it does not announce itself for hours — the hood goes down before the arc is struck. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Deploy Bay (Trade Skills · `devops`)

**Category:** Trade Skills Simulator  
**Trade:** Platform engineer / SRE  
**Union:** Non-union profession (CWA and the Alphabet Workers Union organise some technology workplaces)  
**Certification / standard claimed:** CNCF Certified Kubernetes Administrator (CKA) and AWS Certified DevOps Engineer; NIST SP 800-53 AC-6 least privilege for automation identities; SRE progressive-delivery and rollback practice  
**Tagline:** Environment promotion, agent permission scoping, and a canary-gated rollout on a live deploy pipeline  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the deploy ticket | Select | An agent runs exactly what it's told. You're the one who has to know what that is before it starts. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Confirm the target environment | Select | Staging and production share the same tooling and look the same on a bad night. The label is the only thing standing between a test and an outage. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Verify environment-scoped secrets | Sequence (any order: database credential, API key, webhook secret) | A secret scoped to the wrong environment isn't a mistake you notice — it's a live production credential sitting somewhere it can leak. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Issue a least-privilege token | Select | Least privilege isn't paperwork. It's the difference between a bad deploy and a bad deploy that could also read every customer record. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Review the agent's proposed change | Select | Approving automation output you haven't read is how a bad change ships with a human's name on the approval. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Open the canary to 10% | Turn | You test a rollout on a fraction of traffic before betting all of it. The canary is what tells you whether this build is safe. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Watch the canary error rate | Graded reading | A canary that's already unhealthy at 10% traffic is a full outage at 100%. This is the checkpoint that catches it first. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Promote to full traffic | Turn | Promotion is a deliberate act, separate from opening the canary — the two decisions shouldn't share one motion. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Arm and test the rollback | Timed hold | A kill switch you haven't tested isn't a kill switch — it's a hope. Confirm it works while you don't yet need it for real. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Log the change and the agent's actions | Select | Months from now, someone will need to know exactly what an automation did here and who authorised it. This is that line. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `prod-console` | That tab is the production console, not staging — it renders identically at a glance, which is exactly how a 2 a.m. mistake happens. Close it and confirm you're back in staging before touching anything. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `prod-db-secret` | That credential is scoped to production, not staging. Handing an automated job a secret for the wrong environment lets a test run read or write real customer data. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `admin-token` | That's a standing admin credential, not a scoped token for this job. An automation only needs the access this run requires — a broad, long-lived key turns any bug in its logic into an account-wide blast radius. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Rough-In Bay (Trade Skills · `plumbing`)

**Category:** Trade Skills Simulator  
**Trade:** Plumber / Pipefitter  
**Union:** UA — United Association of Plumbers, Pipefitters and Service Technicians  
**Certification / standard claimed:** UA journeyman plumber and state plumbing licence; IPC/UPC drain-waste-vent code; ASSE 5110 backflow prevention assembly tester; NFPA 51B hot work for torch brazing  
**Tagline:** DWV rough-in, backflow prevention, torch brazing and a pressure test with no shortcuts  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the rough-in work order | Select | You confirm what's supposed to be behind this wall before you cut into it — a vent sized wrong is a sewer-gas complaint six months from now, not something you catch by eye today. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Close the main shutoff | Turn | Every fixture on this run can still be under pressure. The main goes off first — not just the branch you think you're isolating. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Bleed the line | Timed hold | Closing the main doesn't empty the pipe. Whatever's still standing in the line comes out at the first cut unless you bleed it here. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Dry-fit the DWV run | Sequence (strict order: vent stack, closet bend, P-trap) | DWV assembles top-down: the vent connection and slope have to be right before you commit a trap that depends on both. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Fit the backflow preventer | Carry and place | A hose bib is exactly the connection code requires backflow protection on — anything that could ever sit in a bucket needs an assembly that stops the flow from reversing. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Solvent-weld the PVC joint | Timed hold | The solvent weld needs sustained pressure to fuse. Let go early and the pipe's own spring-back opens a gap the cement never fills. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Dial a neutral flame | Graded reading | A neutral flame gives a clean, even heat for sweating copper — too rich soots the joint, too lean burns the flux before the solder can wet the fitting. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Sweat the copper joint | Graded reading | Capillary action pulls molten solder through a properly heated joint on its own — chasing it into a cold or overheated fitting is how you get a joint that looks sound and weeps at the first pressure test. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Hold the test pressure | Graded reading | Code requires the system to hold a defined test pressure for a set duration — a slow bleed that isn't caught here becomes a warranty callback after the drywall's up. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Find the weeping joint | Find (any order: weeping joint) | A pressure drop tells you something's wrong somewhere on the run — it doesn't tell you which joint. That's still a visual check, every time. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `hose-bucket` | That garden hose runs from the hose bib straight into the mop bucket, submerged. Let street pressure drop for a second and that bucket water siphons back into the supply — an air gap or a backflow preventer is what stops it, not hoping the pressure never drops. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `torch-stud` | That torch tip is inches from a bare wood stud with no heat shield behind the joint. Sweating a fitting against dry framing is exactly how a slow smoulder starts inside a wall — one nobody notices until hours after you've packed up. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `pressurized-union` | That union is still showing line pressure on the gauge. Crack a fitting under pressure and whatever's behind it — water, or a still-hot line — comes out at whoever's hands are on the wrench. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `solvent-torch` | The primer and cement are open right next to a lit torch. Both are flammable solvents sitting open — vapour finding that flame is not a maybe, it's a when. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

# Surface Prep & Coatings

## Wash-Down Yard (Trade Skills · `pressure-washer`)

**Category:** Surface Prep & Coatings  
**Trade:** Laborer — surface prep  
**Union:** LIUNA — Laborers' International Union of North America  
**Certification / standard claimed:** LIUNA — laborers' surface-prep & pressure-washing training; stormwater pollution-prevention BMPs (Clean Water Act NPDES, local stormwater ordinance)  
**Tagline:** Contained pressure-wash of a painted wall and apron: nozzle, pressure, standoff, coverage, recovery  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the job sheet | Select | The sheet says stucco and painted trim, a 1,500 psi ceiling, and zero discharge to the storm system. Every setting on the machine follows from those three lines — you read them before you touch the pump. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Seal the storm drain | Carry and place | The drain inside the apron takes everything that runs across it straight to the Bay. It gets sealed before the first drop of water hits the wall — not after you notice the runoff heading for it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Set the containment berms | Sequence (strict order: upstream berm, downstream berm) | Upstream first stops clean runoff from the slope joining your wash water and doubling what you have to recover. Downstream closes the box. Set the low side first and the water walks around it while you're still rolling out the high side. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Put on face shield and boots | Sequence (any order: face shield, rubber boots) | Blow-back off stucco carries grit and paint flake at eye height, and the apron is going to be a sheet of water. Both are on before the pump is, every time. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Walk around the machine | Sequence (strict order: hose couplings, trigger safety lock, nozzle rack) | A coupling that lets go under pressure whips a hose; a trigger without a working lock fires when the wand's set down. Both are a ten-second check that the whole job depends on. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Fit the green 25° tip | Select | A 25° fan spreads the same flow across a stripe wide enough to clean without cutting. The 15° yellow is for bare concrete; the 0° red is on the rack as a trap, and it stays there. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Set the unloader | Graded reading | The machine will make 3,000 psi. The wall is rated for half that — the unloader is where you decide the surface gets cleaned rather than stripped. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Set your standoff | Graded reading | Distance is the other half of pressure. The same tip at six inches etches stucco and at three feet just wets it — the graded band is the distance that cleans. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Make the wash pass | Tracked hold | Coverage is a rhythm: each stripe half over the last, at a speed the tip can actually lift the grime. Rush and it tiger-stripes; dwell and the fan starts cutting. The gauge reads your overlap — hold it in the band. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Recover the wash water | Timed hold | Contained water still isn't disposed of. It goes into the recovery tank for the sanitary connection or the hauler — not left to evaporate the grit onto the apron, and not lifted over the berm. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Walk the berms before sign-off | Find (any order: gap in the downstream berm) | A dry apron inside the box doesn't prove the box held. You walk the berms and look for the track where water got out — that's what's on the discharge log, not what you assumed. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `nozzle-0` | That's the red 0° tip. On stucco and painted trim it doesn't clean, it cuts — a pencil jet at 3,000 psi scores the surface and blows the finish off in strips. It also injects water through skin. It stays on the rack for this job. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `outlet-box` | You just swept the wand across the exterior outlet box. A weatherproof cover is rated for rain, not a pressure jet — water driven into the box makes a live path back up the wand to your hands. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `downstream-drain` | That drain is outside your berms and it goes to the Bay untreated. Wash water is paint chips, detergent, oil and grit — sending it to a storm drain is a discharge violation, and it's the whole reason the containment gets set before the pump starts. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `ladder-wand` | Running the wand off a ladder. The trigger kicks back at the moment you pull it, and the reaction on a ladder pushes you off the wall. Pressure washing is done from the ground or a platform, never from rungs. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

## Coatings Bay (Trade Skills · `paint-sprayer`)

**Category:** Surface Prep & Coatings  
**Trade:** Painter / coatings applicator  
**Union:** IUPAT — International Union of Painters and Allied Trades; hazmat & environmental crews (LIUNA)  
**Certification / standard claimed:** IUPAT — commercial & industrial coatings applicator; EPA RRP lead-safe work practices (40 CFR 745) on pre-1978 surfaces; HAZWOPER awareness (29 CFR 1910.120) for hazmat & environmental crews  
**Tagline:** Airless spray in a pre-1978 room: lead test, containment, tip, pressure, pattern, coverage, film build  
**Automated preview:** ✅ passed 11/11 checks on 2026-09-18 · **Practitioner sign-off:** ☐ pending

### Procedure as simulated

| # | Step | Kind | Rationale shown to the learner | Reviewer: correct? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Read the coating spec | Select | The spec says 1962 building, waterborne acrylic, a 517 tip and 4–6 mils wet. The build year alone changes the whole first hour of the job — you read it before you touch the wall. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 2 | Test the old paint for lead | Select | Pre-1978 means presumed lead until tested. The swab result decides whether this is a paint job or a lead-safe job with containment, HEPA and a cleaning verification — you can't decide that after the dust is already in the air. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 3 | Contain the room | Sequence (strict order: drop cloth, masking film, door seal) | Floor first, because everything else sheds onto it. Masking next while you can still walk on a clean floor. The door seal last, because it's what turns a masked room into a contained one — sealing it first means walking film in and out through it. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 4 | Respirator and coveralls | Sequence (any order: half-mask respirator, disposable coveralls) | Airless atomises the coating into a mist you breathe, and in a lead-safe job the coveralls are what keep the dust from leaving the room on your clothes. Both go on before pressure does. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 5 | Strain the material | Select | A skin or a dried lump in the can is the tip clog you'll be tempted to clear with a finger ten minutes from now. Straining is how you never get there. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 6 | Fit the 517 tip | Select | The first digit is half the fan width, the last two the orifice. A 211 is a trim tip that'd take all day and stripe; a 619 floods this coating on. The spec named 517 for a reason. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 7 | Set the fluid pressure | Graded reading | Pressure is the lowest setting that gives a clean, tail-free pattern. Too low and the edges finger; too high and you're making more overspray than film. The gauge band is that window. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 8 | Shoot a test pattern | Graded reading | The card tells you what the wall would have. An hourglass pattern means tails; a heavy centre means too close. You fix it on a piece of cardboard, not on the client's wall. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 9 | Spray the wall | Tracked hold | Coverage is overlap and speed together: each pass half over the last, at the speed the tip lays 4–6 mils. Slow down and it sags; speed up and it goes thin and dry. The gauge reads your overlap — hold it in the band. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 10 | Check the wet film | Graded reading | Four to six mils wet is what the spec calls for. Under it, the coat won't hide and won't last; over it, it runs before it flashes. The gauge is the only honest answer — not how it looks under the work light. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| 11 | Walk down the containment | Find (any order: gap in the masking film) | A wall that looks right doesn't prove the containment held. You walk the masking edge and look for drift — that's what's on the job log and the RRP checklist, not what you assumed from inside the mist. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |

### Seeded hazards (unsafe actions)

| Hazard | Consequence shown to the learner | Reviewer: realistic? |
|---|---|---|
| `sander` | You reached for the orbital sander before the paint was tested. This building is pre-1978 — dry-sanding untested paint puts lead dust in the air and on every surface in the room, and RRP makes that a violation before it's a health problem for the crew. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `tip-finger` | You went to clear the clogged tip with your finger over the orifice. An airless gun at 2,000 psi injects paint through skin into the hand — it looks like a pinprick and it's a surgical emergency. Trigger lock on, reverse the tip, clear it that way. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `space-heater` | That's a fuel-fired heater running in a room you're about to fill with atomised solvent-borne coating. An ignition source inside the containment is the one thing that turns a paint job into a flash fire. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
| `window-fan` | The box fan is blowing out the open window. Ventilation on this job is filtered negative air, not a fan pushing overspray and lead dust onto the neighbours' cars and the sidewalk. | ☐ yes ☐ no — |
